Reference

Formula sheet

Every numbered equation in the modules, and the working relations their examples use, with all symbols, exact units and the regime each holds in, preceded by a decision tree keyed by what a problem asks for. Where the class notes give a value, the notes' value is stated; where the literature differs, both are given and the difference is flagged.

Covers M01–M15, with M05a, M05b and M07b Equation numbers as in the module that teaches them Values the notes' first, the literature flagged

Each equation carries the number it has in the module that teaches it: (5a.3) is the third equation of M05a. A relation that two modules both state carries both numbers, and a relation a module states without a number carries its section instead. Every gloss ends with a link to the owning module and the section number; the M05a and M07 links land on the equation itself, and the M05b, M06, M07b and M08 links on its section. The high-k gate dielectric (M07) and the memory capacitor (M07b) have their own sections, §11 and §12; the wells (M08) are §5.2, beside the junction and implant equations they build on.

The unit conventions behind most wrong answers

Centimetres throughout. The course works in CGS-derived units, not SI. The permittivity of free space is ε0 = 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm, not 8.85×10⁻¹² F/m. Capacitance is per unit area in F/cm², doping in cm⁻³, areal densities in cm⁻², charge per unit area in C/cm². Convert every length to centimetres before substituting — 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm, 1 nm = 10⁻⁷ cm, 1 µm = 10⁻⁴ cm — and depletion widths come out in cm. Diffusivities are in cm²/s, so a drive-in's Dt is in cm² and its time comes out in seconds: Plummer's 2.5×10⁴ s is 6.8 h (M08's (8.1)–(8.2)).

The exceptions. The Fowler–Nordheim current (6.1) is SI-native: Eox in V/m, the result in A/m², then 1 A/m² = 10⁻⁴ A/cm². Fed V/cm, its exponent is inflated by exactly 100 and the equation returns zero while the prefactor still looks reasonable. The growth laws carry the units of their fitted constants: Deal–Grove (6.5) and Massoud (6.8) in µm and hours — so Massoud's characteristic length L2 ≈ 7 nm enters as 0.007 µm — Reisman–Nicollian (6.6)–(6.7) in nm and minutes. The gate-leakage ceiling (6.2) is computed in A/µm² and converted with 10⁸ µm²/cm², not 10⁴. The tunnelling probability (7.4) is SI as well, the thickness in m and the barrier in J. The memory-cell capacitance (7b.3) is worked in metres, ε0 = 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m, with capacitance densities in F/m² (80 fF/µm² = 0.080 F/m²), and the Arrhenius step (7b.4) takes k in eV/K and T in kelvin.

Densities are not charges. Interface and oxide charge are quoted as areal number densities in cm⁻², and every equation here needs a charge in C/cm². Multiply by q = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C first. Omitting that step gives an answer wrong by about nineteen orders of magnitude; a unit check catches it, since cm⁻² divided by F/cm² is F⁻¹, not V.

0 · Symbol index

Every symbol in the variable tables, sorted by its typed name. Ctrl+F the typed name (for example phi F, N_i, n_i, Cox); the last column gives its value or links to the equation that computes it. Watch the look-alikes: Ni (interface charges, cm⁻²) against ni (intrinsic carriers, cm⁻³), and φS (silicon work function) against φs (surface potential).

1 · Decision tree

Keyed by what the problem asks for, in the order the modules teach it. The strategy column names the route and the reference answer the module works; the last column links to the equation below and to the module section that owns it.

One problem of every type M01–M06 teach is worked in full, with fresh inputs, on the review page: §2.3 for M01–M03, §3.3 for M04, §4.3 for M05, M05a and M05b, and §5.3 for M06. For M07–M15, every board problem P25–P72 is listed with its numbers and answer on the Chapters 4–9 review page: §2.3 for M07 and M07b, §3.3 for M08, §4.3 for M09 and M10, §5.3 for M11 and M12, §6.3 for M13 and M14, and §7.3 for M15.

Asked for…StrategyEquation
Threshold voltage of a MOSFET Four terms, in this order. (i) φF from the body doping, sign by body type. (ii) φMS read from the work-function graph for the gate and that doping. (iii) Cox, with tox in cm. (iv) Qi = qNi, then Wm, then Qd. Sum with the signs of §2.1. Reference: NA = 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, Ni = 5×10¹¹ cm⁻², 1000 Å, Al gate → −2.24 V. The p-channel twin (ND = 10¹⁵ cm⁻³) is −4.919 V as given in the lecture, flagged in §2.3. (3.1)
M03 §7
How a change of oxide thickness moves VT Only the two charge terms contain Cox, and both scale with tox; φMS and 2φF do not change. Halving the 1000 Å oxide of the reference device takes −2.32 V to −1.16 V and +0.402 V to +0.201 V, so VT goes from −2.24 V to −1.28 V. (3.1), (3.4)
M03 §9.2
Implant dose for a required threshold shift A dose D (cm⁻²) of dopant placed at the surface adds a sheet charge qD, which shifts VT by qD/Cox — acceptors (boron) to the positive side. So D ≈ ΔVT·Cox/q, with Cox in F/cm² and q in C. Reference: 0.55 V through 10 nm (3.45×10⁻⁷ F/cm²) → about 1.2×10¹² cm⁻² of boron. (3.4)
M02 §8
Oxide capacitance per unit area εrε0/tox with ε0 in F/cm and tox in cm; εox = 3.9 × 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ = 3.45×10⁻¹³ F/cm. 1000 Å → 3.45×10⁻⁸ F/cm². For a whole gate, multiply by the gate area Z × L in cm². (3.4)
M03 §2
Fermi potential (kT/q) ln(N/ni) with kT/q = 0.0259 V and ni = 1.5×10¹⁰ cm⁻³; natural logarithm. Write the minus sign for an n-type body yourself. 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ → 0.288 V. (3.8)
M03 §5
Maximum depletion width or depletion charge under the gate |φF| first, then Wm with the factor 4, then Qd = qNWm with the sign of the body. 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ → Wm = 0.867 µm, |Qd| = 1.39×10⁻⁸ C/cm². The answer is in cm; multiply by 10⁴ for µm. (3.6), (3.7)
M03 §4
Work function difference Read φMS from the graph against doping (Al on 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p-type: −0.9 V); the formula φM − φS is the check (−0.80 V). Degenerate polysilicon gates: on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p body −0.85 V (n⁺) and +0.27 V (p⁺); on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ n body −0.27 V (n⁺) and +0.85 V (p⁺); Al on that n body −0.22 V. (3.3)
M03 §1.2, §8
Wavelength from a band gap or work function, or the reverse E [eV] = 1.24/λ [µm]. Longer wavelengths than this are not absorbed. Si, 1.12 eV → 1.107 µm; GaN, 3.4 eV → 0.365 µm. (3.2)
M03 §1.1
Net doping after a compensating implant Bring both concentrations to the same power of ten, then subtract: 10¹⁶ − 10¹⁵ = 10×10¹⁵ − 1×10¹⁵ = 9×10¹⁵ cm⁻³, not 10¹. The larger species sets the type. M03 §9.1
Implanted concentration at a depth, or the peak from a dose A Gaussian about Rp with standard deviation ΔRp; the peak is Cp = Q/(√(2π) ΔRp) for a dose Q in cm⁻². (3.9)
M03 §9.2
Whether an implant into bare silicon forms two junctions A second, shallow junction exists when the surface concentration falls below the background: Rp > ΔRp √(2 ln(Cp/NB)). A peak 10⁴ times the background needs Rp > 4.3ΔRp. The lecture's remedy: implant through an oxide about Rp thick, which puts the peak at the interface. (3.10)
M03 §9.2
Junction depletion width, or the minimum channel length or body doping against punch-through Vbi from (2.5), then W from (2.4) with VR the drain voltage, then L > 2W. NA = 10¹⁶, ND = 10²⁰ cm⁻³, 5 V → Vbi = 0.933 V, W = 0.880 µm, L > 1.76 µm. Reverse: W ∝ NA−1/2, so a 1 µm gate at 5 V needs W < 0.5 µm and NA ≈ (0.880/0.5)² × 10¹⁶ ≈ 3.1×10¹⁶ cm⁻³. Heavier body doping narrows W and suppresses punch-through. (2.5), (2.4), (2.3)
M02 §5.2
Peak field at the drain junction Emax = 2(Vbi + VR)/W for a one-sided junction. At 5 V: 4.2×10⁴ V/cm at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, 4.3×10⁵ V/cm at 10¹⁷ cm⁻³. M02 §6.1
Oxide thickness for a gate length tox = LE/45, both in one unit. The notes' points: 1 µm → 200 Å, 0.18 µm → 40 Å, below 0.1 µm → 20 Å. (2.2)
M02 §4
Oxide field, the maximum voltage an oxide takes, or the thinnest oxide for a supply Eox = V/tox with tox in cm; at 10 MV/cm each nanometre holds 1 V. 5 nm at 5 V → 10 MV/cm. A 2 nm oxide caps at 2 V (10 MV/cm) or 1.6 V (8 MV/cm); with about 30% margin, 1.4 V or 1.1 V — say which end of 8–10 MV/cm was taken. Reverse, at the ~5 MV/cm service field: tox ≥ V/Eox, so 1.2 V needs 2.4 nm, which must then survive 8 MV/cm, about 1.9 V. (5.2)
M05 §4.2
Drain current Check the regime first: linear while VD < VG − VT, saturated beyond it. Z is the channel width. (1.4), (1.5)
M01 §4
Etch undercut from a wet etch An isotropic wet etch undercuts the mask by about the depth etched, on each side: a 2 µm window etched through 1 µm of oxide opens to about 4 µm. A dry anisotropic etch leaves it near 2 µm. M01 §7.3
Area or die-count gain from a larger wafer Ratio of the squared diameters: 200 → 300 mm is 2.25×. (4.4)
M04 §4
Pull-rate penalty for a larger crystal The notes' v ∝ 1/D gives a 33% slower pull from 200 to 300 mm; the heat balance gives v ∝ D−1/2 and 18%. Flagged in §2.3. (4.3)
M04 §3
Ionic character of a bond ΔX from the electronegativities of §16.3, then I = 1 − exp(−ΔX²/4). Si–O: 1.54²/4 = 0.593 → 45%. (5a.1)
M05a §1
Atoms or bonds per cm² of a silicon surface 2/a² for (100), 4/(√3a²) for (111), with a = 5.431×10⁻⁸ cm. (100): 6.8×10¹⁴ atoms, two outward bonds each, 1.36×10¹⁵ bonds/cm². (111): 7.8×10¹⁴ of each. (5a.2)
M05a §2
Silicon consumed by an oxidation, the step left when the oxide is stripped, or the oxide needed to consume a given thickness xSi = 0.46 xox in any one unit; 0.54 xox stands above the original surface. 1 µm → 0.46 µm; 2 µm → 0.92 µm; 25 nm → 11.5 nm; stripping a 100 nm oxide leaves the surface 46 nm below where it was. Reverse: xox = xSi/0.46, so 0.23 µm of silicon needs 0.50 µm of oxide. Atoms consumed per cm²: nSixSi. (5a.3)
M05a §4
Threshold shift from a dangling-bond fraction Nit = f × 6.8×10¹⁴ cm⁻² (the notes apply the fraction to the (100) atom count), Qit = qNit, Cox = εox/tox, |ΔVT| = Qit/Cox. One in 1000 through 20 nm → 6.8×10¹¹ cm⁻², 1.09×10⁻⁷ C/cm², 1.73×10⁻⁷ F/cm², 0.63 V. One in 10⁴ → 0.063 V. One in 2000 through 5 nm → 0.079 V. |ΔVT| ∝ f × tox. M05b §3.3, (5b.2)
M05b §3.3
Threshold shift from any oxide or interface charge ΔVT = −Qox/Cox with Qox = q × (areal density). Positive charge gives a negative shift. Charge a distance x from the gate counts x/tox of its value: exact for Qit, close for Qf, an upper bound for Qot and Qm. (5b.2)
M05b §3.3
Which oxide charge a measured threshold drift is Reverses with bias polarity and is accelerated by temperature → Qm. Progressive under electrical stress, no recovery, C–V curve stretched along the voltage axis → Qit from hot carriers breaking Si–H, possibly with some Qot. A rigid shift set at manufacture and dependent on orientation → Qf. (5b.1)
M05b §6, §7
Interface-trap density per unit energy, or what traps do to a C–V curve Dit = (1/q) dQit/dE in cm⁻² eV⁻¹. Cit = q²Dit sits in parallel with CD, and that pair in series with Cox, so traps stretch the curve along the voltage axis and never lift it above Cox. With Dit in cm⁻² eV⁻¹, Cit = q × Dit numerically. (5b.3), (5b.4)
M05b §3.5, §3.7
Which anneal, at what temperature and in what ambient, removes which charge Qit: hydrogen anneal, 400–450 °C, 100% H₂ or 4% H₂ in N₂ (notes), after metallization. Qot: above 550 °C (notes), N₂ or Ar, before metallization. Qf: high-temperature inert anneal, argon preferred. §16.5
M05b §4.1, §5.4
Which tunnelling mechanism operates Fowler–Nordheim needs the triangular barrier inside the film: tox > φox/Eox (3.9 nm at 8 MV/cm), equivalently Vox > φox ≈ 3.1 V. Otherwise the transport is direct tunnelling: in the notes, below 4 nm, and very large below 3 nm. F–N is identified by a straight line of log(J/Eox2) against 1/Eox. (6.1)
M06 §1.2, §1.3
Fowler–Nordheim current at a given field Evaluate (6.1) in SI: Eox in V/m, J in A/m², then ×10⁻⁴ for A/cm²; m* = 0.5m0. Check: 10 MV/cm → 1.8×10⁻⁴ A/cm². Measured at 8 MV/cm: about 5×10⁻⁷ A/cm², six decades below the 1 A/cm² ceiling. (6.1)
M06 §1.2
Gate-leakage ceiling Jmax = (Ioff/W)/L in A/µm², then × 10⁸ µm²/cm². 10 nA/µm and L = 1 µm → 10⁻⁸ A/µm² = 1 A/cm². 100 nA/µm and L = 0.1 µm → 10⁻⁶ A/µm² = 100 A/cm². (6.2)
M06 §2
The thinnest usable gate oxide Tunnelling: excessive below 1.4 nm (notes). Reliability: production 110 Å at 5 V, 65 Å at 3.3 V, 45 Å at 2.5 V, from 80, 45 and 33 Å defect-free at 5.5, 3.6 and 2.75 V plus the notes' 30% margin, which the published column does not follow exactly (§2.3). A DRAM stops near 3 nm; a non-volatile memory tunnel oxide near 8 nm. §16.6
M06 §7
The oxide field at the supply, and the thickness with a defect margin Eox = V/tox, 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm; state which voltage the field is taken at. 5 V on the 80 Å defect-free oxide → 6.25 MV/cm at the nominal supply; 5.5 V, the supply + 10% at which Hu's ten-year criterion is applied, → 6.88 MV/cm. Margin: the notes' 30%, 1.30 × 80 = 104 Å, published as 110 Å, which runs at 4.55 MV/cm at 5 V. Hu's defect-free rows carry 6.25, 7.33 and 7.58 MV/cm at 5, 3.3 and 2.5 V (6.88, 8.0 and 8.3 MV/cm at supply + 10%); the production rows 4.55, 5.08 and 5.56 MV/cm at supply. Holding 6.25 MV/cm would give 52.8 Å at 3.3 V and 40.0 Å at 2.5 V, not 45 and 33 Å. Constant-field check: 1.8 V at 6.25 MV/cm → 28.8 Å; × 1.30 → 37.4 Å, which runs at 4.81 MV/cm at 1.8 V. (5.2), §16.6
M06 §7.1
How much the leakage rises between two thicknesses The ratio of the two current densities at the same bias, as a factor and in decades (log₁₀ of the factor). The notes, at 1 V: 1×10⁻¹² A/cm² at 35 Å and 1×10⁻¹⁰ A/cm² at 15 Å → 10⁻¹⁰/10⁻¹² = 100, two decades over 20 Å. The literature's 15 Å value, about 10 A/cm², makes it 10¹³, thirteen decades; its source calls the span twelve orders, one fewer than its own endpoints give. Either way the current is exponential in thickness. §16.6, §2.3
M06 §7.2
Gate capacitance with polysilicon depletion Qg = εoxEox; Wpd = Qg/(qNp), or εoxVox/(toxqNp) from the oxide voltage; Cpolydep = εsi/Wpd, with εsi = 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm; then 1/Ctot = 1/Cpolydep + 1/Cox, or Ctot/Cox = tox/(tox + tpd) with tpd = εox/Cpolydep = Wpd × 3.9/11.8. Np = 10²⁰ cm⁻³ at 5 MV/cm → Wpd = 1.08 nm, Cpolydep = 9.7×10⁻⁶ F/cm², tpd = 3.56 Å; Ctot/Cox = 0.966 at 100 Å, 0.808 at 15 Å. A p⁺ gate at 5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ on 15 Å → 0.68. The oxide is the smaller capacitor in every case — Cpolydep/Cox = 28 at 100 Å, 4.2 at 15 Å — and so sets most of the total; Cpolydep becomes the smaller only when Wpd exceeds (εsi/εox)tox ≈ 3tox. (6.3), (6.4)
M06 §6.2
How the gate voltage divides once the gate depletes Three drops beyond flat band, Vg − VFB = Vpoly + Vox + φs, with VFB = φMS when the oxide holds no charge: −0.85 to −0.97 V for an n⁺ gate on 10¹⁵–10¹⁷ cm⁻³ p-type silicon, not zero. In strong inversion φs is nearly pinned, so the poly drop comes almost entirely out of Vox, and the inversion charge takes the loss. Reference: 20 Å on 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ under an 8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ gate at Vg − VFB = 2.02 V → Vpoly = 0.093 V, 88 mV from the oxide and 5 mV from the surface; inversion charge 9.6 → 8.7×10¹² cm⁻², −10% (M06's self-consistent solution). (6.9)
M06 §6.1, §6.2
The voltage lost across the depleted polysilicon gate Wpd from (6.4) first, then Vpoly = qNpWpd2/(2εsi) = Qg2/(2εsiqNp), all lengths in cm, q = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C, εsi = 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm. Reference: 1 V on 20 Å (5 MV/cm), Np = 8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ → Wpd = 1.348×10⁻⁷ cm = 13.5 Å, Vpoly = 0.111 V; rounding Wpd to 13 Å before squaring gives 0.104 V, the class's 0.1035 V. Then tpd = 4.5 Å and Ctot/Cox = 0.82. At 10²⁰ cm⁻³ and 5 MV/cm, 0.089 V. Vpoly ∝ Qg2: at a fixed oxide voltage it rises as 1/tox2 (20 → 15 Å at 10²⁰: 0.089 → 0.158 V), and at fixed charge halving the doping doubles it (5×10¹⁹: 0.178 V). (6.10), (6.4)
M06 §6.2
The gate doping that holds Vpoly to a limit Invert (6.10): Np ≥ Qg2/(2εsiqVpoly), Qg = εoxEox. At 5 MV/cm, 50 mV needs 1.8×10²⁰ cm⁻³ (Wpd = 6.1 Å), beyond the about 10²⁰ cm⁻³ n-type polysilicon can hold active; 0.10 V needs 8.9×10¹⁹ cm⁻³. A metal gate does not deplete: Vpoly ≈ 0. (6.10)
M06 §6.2
Why the measured capacitance stays below Cox in inversion at low frequency Polysilicon depletion: Cpolydep in series with Cox. Interface traps stretch the curve along the voltage axis instead, and cannot raise it above Cox. Quantisation of the inversion layer also depresses the curve; the two are separated by fitting a model, not by inspection. (6.3), (5b.4)
M06 §6.2
Why a stressed oxide's current drifts Falling current at constant voltage: net electron trapping near the cathode, which lowers the cathode field — negative feedback. A slow rise: trapped holes raising the cathode field (positive feedback), or trap-assisted current through traps the stress generated; the trace cannot separate them. An abrupt jump: breakdown, soft if the current stays intermediate with a power-law characteristic. The sign of dI/dt does not identify the mechanism. (6.1)
M06 §3, §10
Oxide thickness from Deal–Grove, and which regime applies τ accounts for a starting layer ti: τ = (ti² + Ati)/B. Thin film: tox ≈ (B/A)(t + τ), linear, reaction-limited. Thick film: tox² ≈ B(t + τ), parabolic, diffusion-limited. Valid above 350 Å (notes); dry growth below 200 Å is the anomalous region, where Massoud's term is added. Only B/A depends on orientation. (6.5), (6.8)
M06 §8.1
Thin dry-oxide thickness after a growth time Reisman–Nicollian, in nm and minutes: tox = a(tg + τ)b. At 800 °C (a = 0.302 nm, b = 0.704, τ = 13.1 min), 60 min → 73.10.704 = 20.52 → 6.20 nm, of which 1.85 nm was there at the start. (6.6)
M06 §8.1
The time equivalent τ of an oxide already present Invert the Reisman form at tg = 0: τ = (toxi/a)1/b, in minutes when a was fitted in minutes. 1.0 nm at 800 °C → τ = 5.48 min, and 60 min of growth then gives 5.74 nm. At 1000 °C (a = 3.02 nm, b = 0.701, τ = 1.26 min), 5 min → 10.9 nm. (6.7)
M06 §8.1
The ramp time of a rapid thermal step Temperature rise divided by the ramp rate. From about 25 °C to 1000 °C at the notes' 50–100 °C/s: 975/100 = 9.75 s to 975/50 = 19.5 s, 10 to 20 seconds; to 900 °C, 8.75 to 17.5 s. §16.6
M06 §8.2
The share of a two-step oxynitride grown in the nitriding step, or whether a recipe's step times can grow its thicknesses Share: the step's growth over the final thickness. The notes' furnace recipe, 50 Å at 850 °C for 30 min and then 35 Å more in N₂O at 950 °C: 35/85 = 41% of the 85 Å film. Timing: compare each step's time with the time growth needs, from (6.6). Lecture 10 ran the steps for about 30 s each by RTP; 30 s of dry oxidation at 900 °C grows about 0.65 nm from bare silicon, and 5 nm takes about 9 min, longer still in 10% O₂; N₂O steps run 5–40 min (Wolf). The thicknesses belong to the furnace recipe and its times. (6.6), §16.7
M06 §9.3
The energy the boron receives from a BF₂⁺ implant The molecular ion breaks up at the surface and its atoms share its energy in proportion to their masses: boron takes 11/49 (B 11, F 19 each, BF₂ 49). A 2.2 keV BF₂⁺ beam places its boron as a 0.5 keV B⁺ beam would (Wolf); each ion also brings two fluorine atoms, which sputter the surface (about 20% of the dose lost at a nominal 1×10¹⁵ cm⁻², against 10% for 0.5 keV B⁺). §16.7
M06 §9.4
The reaction that deposits tungsten, and where its fluorine goes Reduction of WF₆ by H₂ or SiH₄ at 250–500 °C, not thermal decomposition. Most of the fluorine leaves as HF or SiF₄; the film keeps some, often 1 atomic percent or more, about one fluorine for every six hundred brought in. On the gate as WSix polycide, anneals drive it through the polysilicon to the Si/SiO₂ interface. (6.11)
M06 §9.4
Equivalent oxide thickness of a high-k film, or the physical thickness for a target EOT tox,eq = thigh-k × 3.9/k in any one length unit; reverse, thigh-k = tox,eq × k/3.9. The notes' case: 10 nm at k = 20 → 1.95 nm, "~2 nm" (2.0 nm exactly with SiO₂'s 3.9 rounded to 4, as Lecture 12 worked it; 3.9 is the course value). A 1.0 nm EOT needs 6.15 nm of HfO₂ (k = 24, the notes' table) or 2.31 nm of Al₂O₃ (k = 9). Lecture 15's problem: a 1.4 nm EOT of HfO₂ at k = 30 → 10.77 nm, about 110 Å as rounded in class (11.0 nm returns 1.43 nm); the same EOT is 8.6 nm at k = 24 and 14.4 nm at 40. Use the k the problem states; where it states none, the notes' table value (§16.11), 24 for HfO₂. (7.3)
M07 §3
EOT of a stack with an interfacial layer, or the interlayer read out of a measured EOT Add the layers' SiO₂ equivalents: tSiO₂ + thigh-k(3.9/k), an oxynitride entering with its own k. 0.5 nm SiO₂ under 3.0 nm HfO₂ (k = 24) → 0.5 + 0.49 = 0.99 ≈ 1.0 nm. Reverse, subtract the high-k term from the measurement: 12 Å EOT with 38 Å of HfO₂ → 12 − 6.2 = 5.8 Å, about 6 Å, seen in micrographs; 10 nm of Ta₂O₅ measured at 2.5 nm → 2.5 − 1.56 ≈ 0.9 nm at k = 25 (1.0 nm at 26, 0.7 nm at 22). The interlayer is a floor: under 0.8 nm of SiO₂ a 1.0 nm EOT needs (1.0 − 0.8) × 24/3.9 = 1.23 nm of HfO₂, and 0.7 nm is out of reach at any k. (7.6), (7.5)
M07 §4
Subthreshold swing, or how much more a device leaks off at a larger swing St = 2.3(kT/q)(1 + Cd/Cox) with kT/q = 25.85 mV; the ideal, Cox ≫ Cd, is 59.5 mV/decade (≈ 60). From threshold to zero gate voltage the current falls VT/St decades: at VT = 0.3 V, 300/60 = 5 and 300/80 = 3.75, so the 80 mV/decade device leaks 101.25 ≈ 18 times more. The notes' 80 mV/decade with Ta₂O₅ is a short-channel value, outside what (7.7) describes. (7.7)
M07 §5.1
How far the current falls below threshold for a gate voltage under VT, or the swing from a measured current ratio Each St of gate voltage below threshold divides the current by ten: ID(VT − ΔV) = ID(VT) × 10−ΔV/St, ΔV and St in the same unit. Lecture 12's transistor: VT = 1.8 V, gate at 1.6 V, 80 mV/decade → 200/80 = 2.5 decades, 316 times smaller; at the ideal 59.5 mV/decade, 3.36 decades, about 2300 times; at 100 mV/decade, 100 times. Reverse: 1000 times over 0.24 V is 3 decades, so St = 80 mV/decade, and an ideal device needs 3 × 59.5 = 178.5 mV, about 0.18 V. The swing is a static property of the transfer curve, the inverse of the slope of log ID against VG (Taur & Ning). (7.7)
M07 §5.1
ALD cycles or deposition time for a thickness, or the growth per cycle from a rate t = Ncyc × g; a rate per minute is g times the cycles per minute. The notes' 200 Å/min at Wolf's 4 cycles/s (240 per minute) → g = 0.83 Å/cycle; 38 Å → about 46 cycles, about 11 s. At one cycle per second the same rate needs 3.3 Å/cycle. Measured TMA/H₂O growth: 1.1–1.2 Å/cycle. (7.8)
M07 §7.2
Threshold shift when the gate material changes ΔVT = φM,new − φM,old, work functions in volts: n⁺ poly 4.05, p⁺ poly 5.17, midgap metal (TiN) ≈ 4.6. n-channel, n⁺ poly → midgap: +0.55 V, the notes' "about 0.6 V"; p-channel, p⁺ poly → midgap: −0.57 V, a larger negative threshold. VT = 0.25 V at a 1.0 V supply → 0.80 V, and the overdrive falls from 0.75 V to 0.20 V. Recovering it by counter-doping gives a buried channel. (7.9), (3.3)
M07 §9
Whether a candidate can serve as a gate insulator Beyond the notes, four tests after k: both band offsets above about 1 eV (Ta₂O₅, 0.35 eV, fails); no reaction with silicon to SiO₂ or a silicide (Ta₂O₅ fails; ZrO₂ marginal on later data); amorphous through 1000 °C for 5 s (HfO₂ and ZrO₂ crystallise; silicates, aluminates and nitrogen help, at a cost in k, HfSiO₄ ≈ 11); and a clean interface, hence the notes' one to two atomic layers of SiO₂, which (7.6) then charges in full. §16.11
M07 §5, §6
Charge or electron count in a DRAM cell, or the voltage a count implies Q = CcellV, N = Q/q; 1 fF × 1 V = 1 fC = 6.24×10³ electrons. 30 fF: 150 fC and 9.4×10⁵ at 5 V; 90 fC and 5.6×10⁵ at 3 V; 30 fC and 1.9×10⁵ at 1 V. Reverse, V = Nq/Ccell: the notes' 1–2×10⁶ electrons in 30 fF would need 5.3–10.7 V — the notes' pairing, stated as they give it, does not follow from Q = CV (§2.3). 25 fF at 1.2 V → 30 fC, 1.9×10⁵, 3.3 times less than 30 fF at 3.3 V (99 fC, 6.2×10⁵). Lecture 12's route, 30 fF ÷ q = 1.9×10⁵, carries the unit F/C = 1/V: electrons per volt, the count at 1 V. (7b.2)
M07b §2.3
Capacitor area, capacitance density or EOT for a target cell capacitance Ccell = ε0kA/t = 3.9ε0A/EOT, A the whole surface the dielectric covers; work in SI, ε0 = 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m and 80 fF/µm² = 0.080 F/m². Planar, 30 fF on 10 nm of SiO₂: 8.7 µm². A pedestal a × b, h high, covered on top and sides: ab + 2(a + b)h; 0.15 × 0.45 µm, 0.3 µm high → 0.43 µm², so 30 fF needs 70 fF/µm² (Wolf quotes 80). At 80 fF/µm², EOT = 0.43 nm; t = EOT × k/3.9 → 2.8 nm of Ta₂O₅ (k = 25), 9.9 nm of BST (k = 90). 0.1 × 0.3 µm, 0.4 µm high, 25 fF → 0.35 µm², 71 fF/µm², 0.48 nm, 3.1 nm Ta₂O₅, 11 nm BST. An interfacial SiO₂ adds in series: 10 nm at k = 90 plus 1 nm of SiO₂ takes the EOT from 0.43 to 1.43 nm, 30% of the intended capacitance. (7b.3), (7.6)
M07b §3, §4.1
The trench depth for a target cell capacitance Capacitance density from (7b.3), 3.9ε0/EOT, then the area A = Ccell ÷ density. A trench with a square opening a on a side and depth d has four walls and a floor, A = a2 + 4ad, so d = (A − a2)/(4a). Reference: 30 fF on 10 nm of SiO₂, 3.45 fF/µm² → 8.69 µm²; a 0.5 µm opening → (8.69 − 0.25)/(4 × 0.5) = 4.2 µm, more than eight times as deep as it is wide, 35 times its footprint in area (Lecture 13: trenches about 5 µm deep). 5 nm of SiO₂ and a 0.25 µm opening → 6.91 fF/µm², 4.34 µm², 4.3 µm. Reverse: 5 µm deep on a 1 µm opening is 21 µm², 72.5 fF at 10 nm. (7b.3)
M07b §3.1
Read signal or transfer ratio of a DRAM cell Vs = (Vnode − Vdd/2) × Ccell/(Ccell + Cbitline). 30 fF on a 300 fF bit line → 0.09; on 100 fF → 0.23. At Taur and Ning's ≈ 0.2, a cell at 1 V on a 1 V supply gives (1 − 0.5) × 0.2 ≈ 0.1 V. The signal scales with Ccell. (7b.1)
M07b §1, §2.2
The next technology nodes, the number of generations between two nodes, or how the cell area and capacitance density change per node Lecture 13's rule: each node is about 0.7 times the last, so the cell area scales by 0.7² = 0.49 per node and, with the capacitance held near 30 fF, the capacitance per unit footprint must double, 1/0.49 = 2.0. Over n generations: node × 0.7n, area × 0.49n, capacitance density × (1/0.49)n, 35 times over five. Generations between two nodes: the natural logarithm of their ratio divided by ln 0.7. Reference: 180 nm → 126, 88, 62, 43, 30 nm, against the table's 130, 100, 70, 50, 35 nm, then 21 and 15 nm; from 250 nm, 175, 123 and 86 nm; 130 → 22 nm is ln(22/130) ÷ ln 0.7 = 4.98, five generations (130 × 0.7⁵ = 21.8 nm). M07b §4.4
The temperature step that doubles a CVD rate, or the rate change over a temperature change Reaction-limited regime only: ΔT×2 = kT2 ln 2/Ea, k = 8.617×10⁻⁵ eV/K, T in kelvin. Ta₂O₅ from TAETO, Ea = 1.3 eV, reaction-limited from 450 to 485 °C: at 467 °C = 740 K, 25 K. For two given temperatures, take the ratio of R = R0exp(−Ea/kT) at each; R0 cancels. (7b.4)
M07b §5.1
Titanium fraction of a BST film, or its Ti:A ratio Ti% = [Ti]/([Ba] + [Sr] + [Ti]) × 100, oxygen not counted; one A cation per Ti is 50%. Best capacitance density at about 51%; k falls about 40% from 51% to 53% (Wolf). Ti:A = Ti%/(100 − Ti%): 1.04 at 51%, 1.13 at 53%. Not the x of BaxSr1−xTiO₃, the barium share of the A sites (0.5 < x < 0.7). (7b.5)
M07b §7.3
The mask thickness for a physical etch such as ion milling, or what is left of the mask or the film tmask ≥ tfilm(1 + OE)/S, with S the film's etch rate divided by the mask's and OE the over-etch as a fraction of the clearing time, often 0.1–0.2; the mask left is tmask − tfilm(1 + OE)/S. Lecture 14's cases, at equal rates (S = 1): 1.3 µm of metal on 1.0 µm of BST keeps 0.3 µm (1.2 µm keeps 0.2 µm); 1.0 µm of resist over a 0.5 µm ruthenium etch keeps 0.5 µm; mask and film both 1.0 µm with a 10% over-etch leaves the patterned film 0.9 µm thick, where (7b.6) asks for a 1.1 µm mask. With the selectivities measured in an Ar/CF₄ plasma, 0.7 against resist and 0.6 against platinum, 0.30 µm of BST at a 20% over-etch needs 0.51 µm of resist or 0.60 µm of platinum: a selectivity below 1 needs a mask thicker than the film. (7b.6)
M07b §9.2
The dose of an implanted layer, or the mean doping of the well it is driven into Q = concentration × thickness, in cm⁻³ and cm. A drive-in conserves Q, so the well's mean doping is Q ÷ its depth. Reference: 0.1 µm at 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ → 10¹⁴ cm⁻²; driven to 2 µm, it averages 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, more near the surface and less near the junction (Lecture 15's 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ is flagged). Reverse: a 2 µm well averaging 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ needs 2×10¹² cm⁻², which in the top 0.1 µm is 2×10¹⁷ cm⁻³. (8.1)
M08 §2.1
The drive-in time for a well depth, or the depth after a time Dt = xj2/(4 ln(Cs/CB)), t = Dt/D, Q = Cs√(πDt); natural logarithm, lengths in cm. At a fixed dose, recompute Cs for each time. Reference (Plummer): 3 µm, Cs = 4×10¹⁷ over 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, 1100 °C (D = 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s) → Dt = 3.76×10⁻⁹ cm² (Plummer rounds to 3.7×10⁻⁹), 6.8 h, 4.3×10¹³ cm⁻². The same dose: 12 h → 3.85 µm, 24 h → 5.27 µm, × 1.37 for twice the time, a little under √2 = 1.41 because Cs falls as the dose spreads; 1 µm in 0.65 h, 2 µm in 2.9 h. (8.1), (8.2)
M08 §2.1
Whether a well is deep enough against vertical punch-through Drain depletion into the well from (2.4), plus the well side of the well–substrate depletion from (8.4), each with Vbi + VR; the well junction must lie deeper below the drain junction than the sum. Both junctions at the supply is the deepest depletion, so the sum is a conservative bound. Reference: a 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ well on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ epitaxial layer, p⁺ drain 10²⁰ cm⁻³, 5 V → 0.880 + 0.259 = 1.14 µm (with the drain junction at 0.3 µm, a well deeper than 1.44 µm); 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ → 0.31 µm; 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at 3.3 V → 0.96 µm. The path from the source, its junction at zero bias, closes at 0.349 + 0.259 = 0.61 µm. (8.3), (8.4)
M08 §2.2
The lateral spread of a diffused well, the narrowest well, or what is left between two wells The notes' rules: spread 0.7xj beyond each window edge, and width at least 1.6 times depth. Reference: 3–4 µm deep → 2.1–2.8 µm sideways, wells at least 4.8–6.4 µm wide. A well is the window plus 2 × 0.7xj wide at the surface: a 2 µm window → 3.4 µm at 1 µm deep, 4.8 µm at 2 µm deep; a 4 µm gap between two windows keeps 4 − 2.8 = 1.2 µm at 2 µm depth. Use 0.7 and 1.6:1 as the notes do; Lecture 15's equal lateral and vertical rates are flagged (§2.3). (8.5)
M08 §2.3
The field threshold under a field oxide, after boron segregation, or with a channel stop (3.1) with CFOX = 3.9ε0/tFOX, tFOX in cm; both charge terms scale with the oxide thickness. A growing oxide takes boron (m ≈ 0.3) and lowers the surface doping. Reference (constructed): 0.5 µm, n⁺-poly line, φMS = −(0.56 + φF), Ni = 10¹¹ cm⁻², 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ → −0.907 − 2.32 + 6.98 + 0.695 = 4.45 V; half the surface boron lost, 5×10¹⁵ cm⁻³ → 2.26 V; a 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ channel stop → 14.0 V. Under 1 µm: 9.1, 4.7 and 28 V; under 0.3 µm: 2.6, 1.3 and 8.3 V. The chain's 150 keV boron under 0.3 µm of field oxide leaves about 4×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at the surface, about 7 V. (8.6), (8.7)
M08 §3.1, §6.4
Where a high-energy implant peaks, and what an anneal does to it Read Rp and ΔRp from range tables (SRIM-2013, §16.14): boron 0.79 µm at 300 keV and 1.13 µm at 500 keV, phosphorus 0.73 µm at 600 keV. An anneal widens the Gaussian, σ = √(ΔRp2 + 2Dt), and the peak falls as ΔRp/σ; the centre stays at Rp. Reference: phosphorus 600 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻², 30 min at 1000 °C (D = 1.27×10⁻¹⁴ cm²/s) → √(2Dt) = 0.068 µm, σ = 0.159 µm, peak 8.3 → 7.5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, still at 0.727 µm; 5 min → × 0.98; 30 min at 1100 °C → σ = 0.271 µm, × 0.53. Lecture 16's peak moving from 0.8 to 1.0 µm is flagged. (8.9)
M08 §4.1, §4.3
How far an implanted well spreads under the mask edge Beyond the edge the concentration falls as ½erfc(y/(√2 ΔR⊥)), half its window value at the edge itself. Reference: boron at 500 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻² (ΔR⊥ = 0.18 µm) reaches 0.53 µm under the mask at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ and 0.65 µm at 10¹⁴ cm⁻³, three to four lateral straggles; a diffused well of the same 1.68 µm depth spreads 1.36 µm. The course's figures: lateral straggle at 500 keV ~0.7 µm (notes, Wolf), spread after all processing ~1 µm, against at least 2 µm for a diffused well. (8.8)
M08 §4.2
The n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing saved by retrograde wells Fractional reduction, (old − new)/old. The notes' 1.2 µm technology: 7, 8 and 9 µm → 5 µm is 29, 38 and 44% less. The chained-implant process reports 40% lower: 6 µm → 3.6 µm. The spacing runs from an n⁺ diffusion in the p-well to a p⁺ diffusion in the n-well. M08 §4.5
The peak concentration and depth of each implant of the well chain Cp = Q/(√(2π)ΔRp), ΔRp in cm, at depth Rp. Reference: phosphorus 600 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻², ΔRp = 0.144 µm → 8.3×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ at 0.73 µm; the 150 keV dose doubled to 8×10¹² cm⁻² → 5.1×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ at 0.20 µm. Phosphorus needs about twice boron's energy for the same depth: 600 keV phosphorus 0.73 µm, 300 keV boron 0.79 µm. The full recipe, with each implant's role, depth and peak, is in §16.14. (3.9)
M08 §6.3
Junction capacitance against the doping beneath it Vbi from (2.5), W from (2.4), then Cj = εSi/W per unit area. Reference: n⁺ (10²⁰ cm⁻³) over p at 0 V: 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ → 0.349 µm, 29.9 nF/cm²; 10¹⁷ → 0.114 µm, 91.7 nF/cm²; 10¹⁸ → 0.037 µm, 282 nF/cm². 3.3 V on the 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ junction → 0.743 µm, 14.0 nF/cm². More doping, a narrower depletion region, a larger capacitance. (8.12), (2.4)

M08 §6.1
Whether the parasitic thyristor of CMOS latches, and what a retrograde well does about it Trigger: a transient current that drops about 0.7 V across Rwell or Rsub. With no shunting resistance the loop holds once αnpn + αpnp ≥ 1, equivalently βnpnβpnp ≥ 1; low well and substrate resistances raise the gain needed. A retrograde well works on both: its doping, rising with depth, sets up a field (kT/q)(1/N)(dN/dx) that pushes the holes the pnp's emitter injects back toward it, about 1.8 kV/cm one straggle above the 600 keV peak, and its heavy bottom lowers Rwell. (8.10), (8.11)

M15 §5.2

2 · How solutions work in this subject

2.1 Signs in the threshold equation

Two of the four terms change sign with the device type, and the result of a sign error still looks plausible.

Termn-channel (p body)p-channel (n body)Rule
φMS−− Negative for Al and for n⁺ poly on either body type
−Qi/Cox−− Qi is positive charge in both cases
−Qd/Cox+− Qd < 0 for a p body (ionised acceptors), > 0 for an n body (ionised donors)
+2φF+− φF > 0 for p type, < 0 for n type

Consequence to check against. For an n-channel device the last two terms share a sign and partially offset the first two; in the M03 §7 device they come to +0.98 V together. With an aluminium or n⁺-polysilicon gate a p-channel device has all four terms negative, so its threshold is large and negative, about twice the magnitude of an n-channel device on the same wafer with the same oxide (−4.919 V against −2.24 V in the course pair); with such a gate, a small or positive p-channel threshold has a sign wrong. A p⁺-polysilicon gate makes φMS positive and can legitimately give a small or even positive p-channel threshold (M02 §8). Lecture 3 draws from this asymmetry that the p-channel device set the supply: about six volts in the 1970s and early 1980s in the lecture's account, while the published record gives about −24 to −28 V for the metal-gate p-channel logic of the mid-to-late 1960s (M03 §6).

2.2 Order-of-magnitude checks

  • Cox is 3.45×10⁻⁸ F/cm² at 1000 Å and scales inversely with thickness: 1.73×10⁻⁷ at 200 Å, 3.45×10⁻⁷ at 100 Å, 6.90×10⁻⁷ at 50 Å, 1.73×10⁻⁶ at 20 Å, 2.30×10⁻⁶ F/cm² at 15 Å.
  • φF is 0.288 V at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ and rises by 0.0259 × ln 10 = 0.060 V per decade of doping: 0.347 V at 10¹⁶, 0.407 V at 10¹⁷.
  • Wm is 0.867 µm at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ and falls roughly as the inverse square root of the doping, about 3.2× per decade, less the slow growth of φF. A junction against a 10²⁰ cm⁻³ side at 5 V reverse bias depletes 2.77, 0.880 and 0.280 µm into bodies of 10¹⁵, 10¹⁶ and 10¹⁷ cm⁻³.
  • An interface charge density of 5×10¹¹ cm⁻² through a 1000 Å oxide costs 2.32 V; the shift is linear in density and in thickness. One dangling bond in 1000 on (100) through 20 nm costs 0.63 V.
  • At 10 MV/cm each nanometre of oxide holds 1 V: 5 V across 5 nm, 2 V across 2 nm. A spot 30% thinner than its surroundings carries 1/0.7 ≈ 1.4 times the field.
  • An oxidation consumes 0.46 of its own thickness in silicon: 1 µm of oxide takes 0.46 µm.
  • The Fowler–Nordheim tunnelling distance at 8 MV/cm is 3.1 V ÷ 8×10⁶ V/cm = 3.9 nm, and the measured F–N current there, about 5×10⁻⁷ A/cm², is six decades below the 1 A/cm² leakage ceiling.
  • The ceiling is 1 A/cm² at 10 nA/µm and a 1 µm gate, and scales as (Ioff/W)/L.
  • Polysilicon depletion at 10²⁰ cm⁻³ and 5 MV/cm adds 3.56 Å of oxide-equivalent thickness: 3.4% of the gate capacitance at 100 Å, 19% at 15 Å. The depleted gate takes about a tenth of a volt — 0.089 V there, 0.111 V for 1 V on 20 Å under an 8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ gate — and the oxide remains the smaller capacitor.
  • Hu's production oxides run near 5 MV/cm at their supplies: 110 Å at 5 V is 4.55 MV/cm; the defect-free minima carry 6.25–7.58 MV/cm at supply.
  • A high-k film is physically thicker than its EOT by k/3.9: about six times for HfO₂ at k = 24, 7.7 times at 30, 2.3 times for Al₂O₃. In a stack the SiO₂ interlayer counts in full, so no k takes the EOT below it.
  • The ideal subthreshold swing is 59.5 mV/decade at 300 K. Each swing of gate voltage below threshold divides the current by ten: 0.2 V at 80 mV/decade is 2.5 decades, 316 times. At a 0.3 V threshold, 80 against 60 mV/decade is 1.25 decades, about 18 times, more off current.
  • One femtofarad at one volt holds 1 fC, 6.24×10³ electrons; 30 fF at 1 V holds 1.9×10⁵.
  • ALD adds about an ångström per cycle: 0.83 Å from the notes' 200 Å/min at 4 cycles/s, 1.1–1.2 Å measured for TMA with water.
  • A reaction-limited CVD rate with Ea = 1.3 eV doubles every 25 °C near 467 °C.
  • A trench capacitor's area is its floor plus four walls, a2 + 4ad: 30 fF on 10 nm of SiO₂ needs 8.7 µm², a trench 4.2 µm deep at a 0.5 µm opening.
  • Each technology node is about 0.7 times the last, so the cell area falls to 0.49 and, at constant cell capacitance, the capacitance per unit footprint doubles every node.
  • A physical etch erodes its mask with the film: at equal rates the mask loses what the film loses, plus the 10–20% over-etch, and a selectivity below 1 needs a mask thicker than the film.
  • A drive-in conserves the dose, and depth grows as √(Dt): twice the time gives a little under √2 times the depth (1.37 for Plummer's well), and 12–24 h at 1100 °C makes wells 3–4 µm deep or more, against under an hour for 1 µm. An anneal widens an implanted peak, σ2 = ΔRp2 + 2Dt, without moving it.
  • 300 keV boron and 600 keV phosphorus stop at about the same depth, 0.79 and 0.73 µm, and ions range about 1.8 times as far in photoresist as in silicon.
  • A 0.5 µm field oxide has fifty times less capacitance than a 10 nm gate oxide, so both charge terms of the threshold are fifty times larger: 4.45 V at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³, against −0.1 V for the same surface under the gate oxide.
  • Depletion width falls as N−1/2, so junction capacitance rises as N1/2: ten times the doping beneath an n⁺ junction is about three times the capacitance, 29.9 → 91.7 nF/cm² from 10¹⁶ to 10¹⁷ cm⁻³.

2.3 Where the notes and the literature differ

The notes' value is the course's value, and it is the one this sheet gives first. The right-hand column says what the literature or physics gives, or — where the row says so — what a lecture said that the notes and the modules do not adopt; a lecture value is never the one to use. Each difference below is flagged on the module page named beside it.

Where the notes, the lectures and the literature differ

QuantityCourse valueLiterature, physics, or a lecture statement not adopted
Intrinsic carrier concentration
M01, M02 §5.2, M03 §5		 ni = 1.5×10¹⁰ cm⁻³ (course convention) 9.65×10⁹ cm⁻³ measured; moves 2φF and Vbi by 23 mV
p-channel threshold, ND = 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, 1000 Å, Al gate
M03 §7.1		 −4.919 V (Lecture 3) The stated terms with φMS = −0.215 V give −3.51 V; −4.919 V needs φMS = −1.62 V, which no gate material provides
φMS, Al on 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p-type
M03 §1.2		 −0.9 V from the graph −0.80 V from (3.3) with φM = 4.1 V; the measured polycrystalline Al work function, about 4.28 eV, would give −0.62 V
Supply set by the p-channel threshold
M03 §6		 About 6 V in the 1970s and early 1980s (Lecture 3) Metal-gate p-channel logic of the mid-to-late 1960s ran at about −24 to −28 V; silicon-gate p-channel parts at about 15–16 V; then +5 V
Why the VT implant goes through an oxide
M03 §9.2		 To put the peak at the interface so that only one junction forms (lecture) Textbooks: to suppress channelling and protect the surface. Either way the profile in the silicon is shallower by about the oxide thickness
Polysilicon replaced aluminium
M02 §3.1, M03 §8		 "In the mid-1970s" (notes) Self-aligned silicon gate in production at Fairchild in 1968, mainstream by about 1973
Generation name and gate length
M02 §1, §4		 Treated as one number (notes); 0.18 µm → 40 Å Different from the 0.25 µm generation on (≈200 nm gates); the 130 nm generation shipped 70 nm gates. At 0.18 µm the physical gate was ≈140 nm and the shipped EOT 1.9–2.5 nm. The notes' calendar runs two to four years late from the mid-1990s
Polycide sheet resistance
M02 §3.2		 Reduced "to one fourth" (notes) From 20–30 Ω/□ to 2–5 Ω/□, a factor of five to ten
LDD doping
M02 §6.1, §6.2		 About 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (notes) The notes' own 1–5×10¹³ cm⁻² dose over a 0.15–0.2 µm junction gives roughly 10¹⁸ cm⁻³
Supply at 0.18 µm
M02 §6.2		 Reduced from 5 V to 2 V (notes) Nominal 1.8 V, reached in steps 5 → 3.3 → 2.5 → 1.8 V
Field oxide thickness
M02 §9, M05 §1, M08 §3.1		 About 1 µm (lecture and notes text) The micron-era figure; the notes' own table gives 200–400 nm for LOCOS, and Plummer's flow about 0.5 µm
Czochralski pull rate
M04 §3		 v ∝ 1/D (notes) vmax ∝ D−1/2; 200 → 300 mm costs 18%, not 33%
Electronic-grade purity
M04 §2		 99.9999%, six nines (class) Nine to eleven nines; six nines is solar grade
Reaction temperatures, (4.1)–(4.2)
M04 §2		 1400 °C and 900 °C (class) Carbothermic reduction 1800–2000 °C; SiHCl₃ synthesis about 300 °C; Siemens deposition 1100–1175 °C in H₂
The 8 MV/cm figure
M05 §4.2		 Both the minimum breakdown strength and the field for a 20-year life (notes) Cannot both hold: time to breakdown falls a decade per 0.3–0.5 MV/cm. Read as ≥ 8 MV/cm to be accepted and ~5 MV/cm in service
Requirement 9
M05 §4		 "Asymmetric CMOS" (notes) "Symmetric CMOS" (Wolf, the literature); both mean dual-doped polysilicon gates
O–O distance in SiO₂
M05a §3		 2.27 Å (notes p. 22) The tetrahedral edge from Si–O = 1.62 Å is 2.65 Å; measured 2.62–2.65 Å; 2.27 Å would need an O–Si–O angle of 89°
Silicon consumption ratio
M05a §4		 0.46 (Lectures 8 and 9) Texts give 0.44–0.46 (Plummer 0.45); the spread is the spread in oxide density. Wolf's 2.20 g/cm³ gives 0.44, the 2.27 g/cm³ of Sze & Ng gives 0.46
Atomic layers in a 2 nm oxide
M05a §3, M02 §4		 10–12 (notes) A counting convention, not a structure: 12 by the Si–O bond length, 8–11 by density
Qm and Qf
M05b §1		 "Not well controlled" (notes p. 23) Wolf pp. 82–83: "not only well controlled"; their impact does not grow as oxides scale
The Pb level
M05b §3.2		 "A deep trap level near the Si midgap" (notes) Two charge transitions seen by spin resonance, Ev + 0.31 eV (+/0) and Ev + 0.80 eV (0/−), about 0.25 eV either side of midgap; the C–V interface-trap density peaks at Ev + 0.26 and + 0.84 eV (dry-oxidised (111), Poindexter et al. 1984)
Stretched-bond levels at the interface
M05b §3.5		 A continuum of deep levels (notes) Calculation and the U-shaped Dit place them toward the band edges
What Cit is in parallel with
M05b §3.7		 "The silicon gate capacitance" (notes); "Cox" (Lecture 7) The depletion capacitance CD; that pair is in series with Cox
Forming gas
M05b §4.1		 100% H₂, or 4% H₂ in N₂ (notes); 3% H₂ in 97% Ar (Lecture 7) Forming gas is H₂ in N₂; both mixtures are classed non-flammable; Plummer uses 10% H₂ in N₂
Hydroxyl as a terminator
M05b §4.2		 Released with less energy than hydrogen (Lecture 7) Si–O, 4.68 eV, is stronger than Si–H, 3.30 eV; the sources give no bond-level reason for hydroxyl's poorer record
Qot anneal temperature
M05b §5.4		 Above 550 °C (notes p. 29; Wolf p. 90) Lecture 8 gave about 500 °C in Ar or N₂; the course follows the notes
The symbol Eox
M06 §1.1		 Labels the 3.1 eV barrier energy (notes p. 31) This site writes the barrier as qφox and keeps Eox for the field
F–N current and the need for high-k
M06 §1.2		 Lecture 8: F–N at ~8 MV/cm is comparable to the off-state leakage 5×10⁻⁷ A/cm² is six decades below 1 A/cm²; the notes place the limit on direct tunnelling
φMS of an n⁺ polysilicon gate on p-type silicon
M06 §6.1		 Lecture 11: close to zero, because polysilicon and silicon share a band gap; left out of the voltage division −0.85 to −0.97 V for 10¹⁵–10¹⁷ cm⁻³; near zero only for a gate doped like the body. The lecture's VG is Vg − VFB
Cpolydep as the oxide thins
M06 §6.2		 Constant at fixed poly doping (notes) Constant only at fixed gate charge too; rising oxide fields lowered it, which strengthens the notes' conclusion
Which capacitor is smaller
M06 §6.2		 Cox, much smaller than Cpolydep until the oxide is below about 10 nm (notes); Lecture 11: Cpolydep, which dominates The lecture's own 20 Å example gives Cpolydep = 7.7×10⁻⁶ against Cox = 1.7×10⁻⁶ F/cm²: the oxide is 4.5 times smaller and sets most of the total. Cpolydep is the smaller only when Wpd > 3tox
Where poly depletion starts to matter
M06 §6.2		 Below about 10 nm (notes); Lecture 11: oxides of 2–4 nm Not a conflict: 10 nm is where it first becomes noticeable, 2–4 nm where it is large, 18% of the capacitance at 20 Å
Electron density of a metal gate
M06 §6.2		 Lecture 11: about 10²¹ cm⁻³ 10²²–10²³ cm⁻³ (Cu 8.5×10²², Al 1.8×10²³); either screens the gate charge within about an ångström, so no depletion layer forms
Hu's defect margin
M06 §7.1		 Production thickness = defect-free + 30% (notes) 110, 65 and 45 Å over 80, 45 and 33 Å imply 37.5, 44.4 and 36.4%
The 3.3 V and 2.5 V oxides
M06 §7.1		 45 Å and 33 Å (notes); Lecture 10 read them as following from 5 V on 80 Å, 6.25 MV/cm Holding 6.25 MV/cm gives 52.8 Å and 40.0 Å. The tabulated films carry 7.33 and 7.58 MV/cm at supply: each voltage was extrapolated separately
Leakage at 15 Å and 1 V
M06 §7.2		 1×10⁻¹⁰ A/cm² (notes p. 44): 100 times, two decades, above 35 Å About 10 A/cm²: the source prints "1 × 10" with no exponent. Its "twelve orders" from 35 Å is thirteen by its own endpoints
Deal–Grove lower limit
M06 §8.1		 Valid above 350 Å (notes; Wolf) Lecture 8 said 250 Å
RTO process temperature
M06 §8.2		 800–1000 °C (notes p. 36) Wolf: 800–1050 °C; the notes' p. 35 leakage optimum is 1050 °C; Lecture 9: 1000–1050 °C; Lecture 10: 900–1050 °C
Field for the N₂O-film mobility gain
M06 §9.3		 "Lateral" (notes; Wolf's text) Wolf's figure plots the effective normal, transverse field
Two-step oxynitridation
M06 §9.3		 Furnace: 50 Å of SiO₂ at 850 °C for 30 min, then N₂O at 950 °C for 35 Å more, 85 Å (notes p. 40); Lecture 10: one sequence — O₂ at 900 °C, N₂O at 950 °C, N₂ at 950 °C, cool — about 30 s per step by RTP 30 s of dry oxidation at 900 °C grows about 0.65 nm from bare silicon, and 5 nm takes about 9 min; Wolf's N₂O steps run 5–40 min. The thicknesses need the furnace recipe's times
Low-pressure oxynitridation
M06 §9.3		 1–50 Torr of N₂O (notes) Lecture 10: RTP at 1–20 Torr, nitriding in NO + O₂ at 800–850 °C. The study Wolf reports was a low-pressure furnace
The reaction of the NO + O₂ step
M06 §9.3		 Lectures 9 and 10: 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂ Balanced, but the cold-gas oxidation of NO (ΔG° = −71 kJ/mol at 25 °C), disfavoured above about 490 °C (+47 to +55 kJ/mol at 800–850 °C). At the wafer NO nitrides while O₂ oxidises, as the notes describe for the products of N₂O (p. 41)
How tungsten is deposited
M06 §9.4		 Lecture 10: WF₆ → W + 3F₂ at 700–800 °C WF₆ is reduced by H₂ or SiH₄ at 250–500 °C, (6.11), and the fluorine leaves as HF or SiF₄. Decomposition would cost +1632 kJ/mol at 25 °C, +1406 kJ/mol at 800 °C
Sources of fluorine in the gate oxide
M06 §9.4		 A BF₂⁺ implant and LPCVD-W or WSix (notes); Lecture 10 added reactive ion etching in SF₆, "a Freon" The notes' and Wolf's list has the two routes. Freon is a trade name for halocarbons; SF₆ contains no carbon and is not one
Fluorine dose
M06 §9.4		 Kept below 1×10¹⁴ cm⁻² (notes); Lecture 10: a low dose, tied to the p⁺ source/drain implant The study behind it implanted fluorine into the p⁺ polysilicon gate on purpose, as a separate co-implant, and used 1×10¹⁴ cm⁻² itself, a "medium dose": the source supports "at or below". It is the dose of the added fluorine alone (Chen et al., Electrochem. Solid-State Lett. 3, 290 (2000))
The dual-oxide flow
M06 §9.5		 Implant nitrogen through a screen oxide, strip it, oxidise once (notes p. 42); Lecture 10 kept the first oxide, nitrided by the implant, and oxidised again Both give thinner oxide where nitrogen was implanted, though not the same film. The notes' five steps are the course's
What counts as high-k
M07 §1.1		 k > 7, above Si₃N₄ (notes p. 49) Lecture 11 called silicon nitride and the oxynitrides, k about 4–7 by their nitrogen content, the early high-k films, used in EEPROMs. Others draw the line at 3.9; Robertson asks for above 10
Gate oxide at 0.13 µm
M07 §1.2		 About 3.0 nm in 2001; under 1.5 nm needed at 0.1 µm (notes p. 49) Leading-edge 130 nm logic used 1.5 nm at 1.3 V (2000); SiO₂-based films reached about 1.2 nm at the 90 nm generation (2002). Lecture 12: 3–4 nm at 0.13 µm and 1.5–2 nm at 0.1 µm
The passivating interlayer's thickness
M07 §5.1		 One to two atomic layers of SiO₂ (notes p. 51; Wolf p. 148): 0.17–0.4 nm on the notes' 10–12 layers per 2 nm, 0.35–0.7 nm per SiO₂ unit Lecture 12: 1–2 nm, once 15–20 Å. That much would by itself use up most or all of the EOT below 1.5 nm the notes require at 0.1 µm
ALD growth per cycle
M07 §7.2		 A few ångströms, "as determined by lattice constant"; 50–200 Å/min (notes p. 65) Adsorption saturates below a monolayer (steric hindrance); TMA/H₂O grows 1.1–1.2 Å/cycle. Wolf gives "up to 200 Å/min"; the 50 is the notes' own
ALD "low impurity levels"
M07 §7.2		 Notes p. 65 Wolf's comparison with CVD; Robertson rates ALD purity equal to MOCVD and below sputtered or oxidised-metal films
ALD of SiO₂ from silane
M07 §7.2		 Wolf's example (pp. 171–172), which Lecture 14 used: silane, then O₂ or H₂O, pulsed in turn, the excess pumped out after each pulse Lecture 14 had the silane decompose, SiH₄ → Si + 2H₂, which is CVD and does not stop at a monolayer, and omitted the pump-out. Wolf's chemisorbed silane is itself an idealisation: SiO₂ ALD uses SiCl₄ and H₂O with pyridine (Klaus 1997) or aminosilanes with an O₂ plasma (Fang 2015)
Ta₂O₅ dielectric constant
M07 §8.1, M07b §5		 25 (pp. 54, 58); 26 (p. 49 table); 22 (p. 56 roadmap) All three inherited from Wolf; Robertson 22, Wilk 26; a film crystallised on Ru reached 65
Nitriding the Ta₂O₅ interlayer
M07 §8.1		 2 nm oxide, N₂ anneal at 800 °C for 30 [min] (notes p. 59) N₂ is nearly inert toward SiO₂ at 800 °C; nitridation uses NH₃, N₂O or NO
"1000× smaller leakage"
M07 §8.1		 NO interlayer, 800 °C for 20 s, then CVD Ta₂O₅, EOT 2.3 nm (notes p. 60) Against the same Ta₂O₅ on an SiO₂ interlayer (Wolf p. 161), not against an SiO₂ gate
Al₂O₃ band gap
M07 §8.2		 8.7 eV (p. 49 table); ≈ 8.3 eV (p. 66) 8.8 eV bulk; about 6.8 eV for ALD films (Robertson)
Al₂O₃ precursors
M07 §8.2		 TMA and ozone at 450 °C (notes p. 66; ALD in Wolf); k = 9 Lecture 15: TMA with O₂ by MOCVD, and "aluminium tetrachloride" with O₂, "mixed in water", by ALD. The chloride is AlCl₃, a solid whose vapour is pulsed with water vapour, 2AlCl₃ + 3H₂O → Al₂O₃ + 6HCl; it hydrolyses violently in liquid water. Lectures 11 and 15: k 9–11
ZrO₂ band gap
M07 §8.3		 7.8 eV (p. 49 table); ≈ 5.1 eV (p. 66) 5.8 eV, the same as HfO₂ (Robertson); 7.8 eV is an early value
ZrO₂ lattice mismatch with Si
M07 §8.3		 2.1% (notes p. 66) 6.6% from 5.43 and 5.07 Å (Robertson); it matters only for epitaxial films
ZrO₂ on silicon
M07 §5.3, §8.3		 "Thermodynamically stable with Si" (notes p. 66) Later data: slightly unstable, can form ZrSi₂ (Robertson 2004); HfO₂ is the stable member of the pair
ZrO₂ post-deposition anneal
M07 §8.3		 400–600 °C (notes) Wolf: 400–500 °C for the anneal that reduces leakage; 400–600 °C is his range for significant SiO₂ growth at the interface
HfO₂ dielectric constant
M07 §8.4		 24–40 (p. 66); 24 (p. 49 table) 20–25 (Taur & Ning), 25 (Wilk; Robertson); higher values belong to the tetragonal and cubic phases. A calculation uses the k the problem states — Lecture 15 quoted 25–30 and worked its problem at 30 — and the table's 24 where none is stated
HfO₂ "no interfacial layer"
M07 §8.4		 With the polysilicon deposited below 600 °C and 1000 °C RTA (notes pp. 66–67) The gate side only: about 6 Å of SiO₂ forms at the substrate, inside the 12 Å EOT. Lecture 15 put the 600 °C on the HfO₂ deposition itself (possibly 200–300 °C); in the notes and Wolf (p. 175) it is the polysilicon gate's
HfO₂'s four decades less leakage at 12 Å EOT
M07 §8.4		 Notes p. 67 Wolf (p. 176): from the physical thickness, 38 Å against 12 Å. Lecture 15 attributed it to the absence of the top interfacial layer
Hafnium silicate's k
M07 §8.5		 Not given; HfSiON named (notes p. 67) Lecture 11: a mixture of HfO₂ and SiO₂ at 20–25; Lecture 15: HfO₂ doped with a very small amount of silicon. A k that high is the hafnium-rich end; HfSiO₄ is about 11 (Robertson)
Electrons per 30 fF cell
M07b §2.3		 1–2×10⁶ (notes p. 52; Wolf p. 150) Q = CV: 1.9×10⁵ at 1 V, 5.6×10⁵ at 3 V, 9.4×10⁵ at 5 V; 1–2×10⁶ needs 5.3–10.7 V
Memory size
M07b §3.3		 "256 MB", "4 GB" (notes p. 54) Megabits and gigabits: the 256 Mb and 4 Gb generations
Conducting nitride electrodes
M07b §4.1		 TaN and TiN, "near metallic" (notes p. 55) Lecture 13 called TiN, WN and TaN semi-metallic and put their conductivity very close to aluminium's. Thin-film TiN is 50–150 µΩ·cm against 2.7–3.0 µΩ·cm for aluminium, 17 to 56 times higher (Plummer Table 11-2)
RuO₄
M07b §4.3		 MP 25.4 °C; "flamable and toxic" (notes p. 64) Melting point and toxicity right; a powerful oxidiser, not flammable
The DRAM capacitor roadmap
M07b §4.4		 Ta₂O₅ at k 22 and 50; "?" for 2011 and 2014 (notes p. 56) Lecture 13 dictated Ta₂O₅ at 25 in both columns and filled the "?" with SBT at k = 1500. SBT is the FeRAM ferroelectric, whose films reach a few hundred, and a DRAM dielectric must not be ferroelectric
Ta₂O₅ CVD temperature and method
M07b §5.1		 TAETO or TATDMAE with O₂, 1–4 Torr, 450–485 °C, the method unnamed (notes p. 58); LPCVD in Wolf Lecture 13: 480–500 °C, running past the 485 °C edge of the reaction-limited, conformal window; it called the process MOCVD (at 1–4 Torr with a metal-organic precursor it is both) and added a tantalum-halide LPCVD route that is not in the notes
The interlayer under Ta₂O₅
M07b §5.2		 "An interfacial layer of SiO₂ that is achieved by rapid thermal (polysi) nitridation" (notes p. 58): one layer in two steps, the polysilicon oxidised and the oxide then nitrided by RTA in N₂O (Lecture 13) Not a conflict but a second recipe: Wolf (p. 156) nitrides the bare polysilicon in NH₃, for example at 900 °C for 60 s, to eliminate such an oxide. Either nitrided layer retards further oxidation rather than stopping it (Wolf p. 118)
Ta₂O₅ densification
M07b §5.3		 O₂ anneal or oxygen RTA at 600–700 °C (notes p. 58) Lecture 13: 900–1000 °C, wholly above Wolf's ceiling of about 800 °C (p. 157), past which silicon diffuses through the nitrided interlayer and the SiO₂ thickens
Bottom electrode for Ta₂O₅
M07b §5.4		 Pt or RuO₂ (notes p. 59) Wolf's reports: TiN or RuO₂ bottom; Pt only as a top electrode
Al-doped Ta₂O₅, 0.9Ta₂O₅–0.1Al₂O₃
M07b §5.5		 k = 45 (notes p. 59) 42 (Wolf p. 159); 42.8 at 100 kHz in a 2003 study. Lecture 13: 45–50
Ta₂O₅'s last DRAM generation
M07b §5.5		 1 Gb and 4 Gb (0.15 µm), with HSG (notes p. 59) Wolf's next sentence: the limit "may be reached at the 1-Gb integration level"; minimum EOT about 1.5 nm
BST dielectric constant
M07b §7.1		 300 (p. 49 table); 250 (p. 56 roadmap); 90 (p. 60, DRAM films) Not a conflict: a tabulated value, a requirement and a film value; Wolf 88–90 for films of DRAM thickness. Lectures 11 and 14 gave 100–400, and Lecture 13 about 1500, calling BST ferroelectric; DRAM BST is used paraelectric (Wolf p. 163). Both lecture figures lie inside the notes' few hundred to few thousand for the family (p. 54); the flag is on the label
BST band gap
M07b §7.1		 None given in the notes Lecture 11: 5–6 eV. The titanates' gaps are near 3 eV, SrTiO₃ 3.2 eV (Robertson), with a conduction-band offset to silicon near zero
BST dispersion
M07b §7.2		 Small "because of the high-k value" (notes p. 60) Two independent merits (Wolf p. 162); the 2% and 7% stand, as upper bounds
BST CVD precursors
M07b §8.2		 β-diketonate adducts of barium and strontium with an organometallic titanium compound, in an organic solvent (notes p. 62) Lecture 14: BaCl₂, SrCl₂ and TiCl₄ in water. The barium and strontium chlorides melt only above 850 °C and have no useful vapour pressure near 500 °C; TiCl₄ boils at 136 °C but hydrolyses in water
BST deposition window
M07b §8.3		 480–550 °C, conformality 80–100% (notes p. 62) Two regimes: 480 °C, non-adducted precursors, 100%; about 550 °C, adducted, crystalline but poorly conformal. Lecture 14's 500–700 °C MOCVD furnace is the notes' anneal range
Aluminium excluded for BST
M07b §9.1		 Process steps above 600 °C (notes p. 63) Wolf: above 500 °C; either excludes Al, which melts at 660 °C — from the top electrode too, since the BST anneal follows it
Pt as a BST top electrode
M07b §9.1		 First in the notes' list (p. 64) Not in Wolf's list: Pt catalyses the reduction of BST in the forming-gas anneal
BST dry etch
M07b §9.2		 No volatile by-products, so the etch must be physical, like ion milling (notes p. 64; Wolf p. 168) Lecture 14: CF₄ reactive ion etching, every product volatile. Only TiF₄ is volatile (it sublimes at about 284 °C); BaF₂ and SrF₂ melt above 1350 °C and boil above 2100 °C, and measured fluorine-plasma etches leave them behind
The ion-milling beam
M07b §9.2		 Ion milling; small features hinder it (notes p. 64) Lecture 14: a beam focused to about 1 µm. An ion mill's beam is broad and the mask sets the pattern; a focused beam is a separate tool, the focused ion beam (Plummer p. 636)
The Ru/BST/Ru flow
M07b §9.3		 SiO₂ hard mask; two-step BST CVD, buffer and main layer each annealed; top Ru the common cell plate (notes p. 64; Wolf p. 163) Lecture 14: a thick resist mask, 1 µm for a 0.5 µm etch; an SiO₂ "window" after the Ru etch; one BST deposition at 500–550 °C and one anneal at 600–700 °C; the top Ru patterned as the word line. Its second plug mask is flagged too: plugs are made by blanket fill and etch-back or CMP (Wolf p. 677 and Plummer p. 721, for tungsten)
SBT
M07b §10.1		 "Strontium bismuth titanate"; k = 1000–1500, with PZT (notes p. 57) SrBi₂Ta₂O₉, a tantalate; 1000–1500 is PZT's value (Wolf); SBT films about 180–300
The diffused well's profile
M08 §2.1		 "Drive in after drive-in is highest at the surface" (notes p. 70) Wolf: the doping concentration after drive-in, highest at the surface and falling monotonically to the junction
A drive-in's numbers
M08 §2.1		 12–24 h above 1100 °C; wells 3–4 µm deep (notes pp. 68–69) Lecture 15: a 0.1 µm layer at 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ driven to about 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ in a 2 µm well, 12 h for 1 µm and 24 h for 2 µm. That layer averages 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ over 2 µm; depth grows as √(Dt), so twice the time gives about 1.4 times the depth, and 1 µm takes under an hour
What prevents vertical punch-through
M08 §2.2		 A deep drive-in (notes p. 68) Lecture 15 answered that the depth does; depth and doping both decide it. The clearance needed at 5 V is 1.14 µm at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ and 0.31 µm at 10¹⁷ cm⁻³, because both depletion widths shrink as the doping rises
Lateral diffusion of a well
M08 §2.3		 About 0.7 times the junction depth (notes p. 69) For a Gaussian drive-in under a sharp mask edge, 0.77–0.87 of the junction depth for surface-to-background ratios of 10²–10⁴ (M08's two-dimensional solution). Lecture 15: lateral and vertical at the same rate
Maximum aspect ratio of a diffused well
M08 §2.3		 About 1.6:1; "This would intermingle with the dopant of adjacent wells" (notes p. 69) Wolf supplies the condition the notes drop: wells narrower than about 1.6 times their depth intermingle. M08's reading: 1.6 = 2 × 0.8, so the two figures agree at a lateral factor near 0.8 (2 × 0.7 = 1.4); 1.6 may equally be 1.4 with a margin
Boron lost to the field oxide
M08 §3.2		 The p-well's boron "segregates into the oxide" (notes p. 69) Lecture 16 called it boron penetration and said it changes the field oxide's electrical properties. Boron penetration is the gate's problem, boron from a p⁺ gate crossing the gate oxide (M02 §8, M06 §9.1); the field threshold falls because the silicon beneath the oxide loses boron
Depth of the retrograde peak
M08 §4.1		 The p. 70 sketch puts it at about 0.6 µm Wolf: roughly 1 µm for the energies most used; SRIM-2013 gives 0.73–0.79 µm for the recipe's well implants and 1.13 µm for boron at 500 keV. A sketch, not a value
Lateral straggle at 500 keV
M08 §4.2		 ~0.7 µm (notes p. 70, from Wolf); spread after all processing ~1 µm As a standard deviation, 0.18 µm for boron at 500 keV (SRIM-2013), about a quarter of 0.7 µm. M08's reading: 0.7 µm is the size of the sideways reach of the doping to the background, three to four standard deviations
What the activation anneal does
M08 §4.3		 Very little additional diffusion; a short anneal at a relatively low temperature (notes p. 70) Lecture 16: the peak moves deeper, from about 0.8 to 1.0 µm, and the top layer becomes amorphous. Diffusion spreads a buried peak symmetrically, so it stays at Rp; the well dose is about thirty times below the roughly 10¹⁵ cm⁻² at which an implant amorphises silicon
A drive-in after the retrograde implant
M08 §4.3		 None: a short activation anneal (notes p. 70) Lecture 16: a drive-in to 2–3 µm, the peak falling from 10¹⁹ to about 10¹⁷ cm⁻³. The notes' 3×10¹³ cm⁻² well implants peak near 8–9×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ as implanted, and a long drive-in would undo the retrograde shape
Retrograde wells and LOCOS
M08 §4.4		 Implanted after the LOCOS field oxide is grown (notes p. 71); the p. 74 sketch implants through it Lecture 16: LOCOS is not performed for the retrograde structure. The notes date LOCOS's end to the 0.35 µm generation (p. 11), when trench isolation replaced it for its own reasons
The spacing retrograde wells shrink
M08 §4.5		 n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing, 7–9 → 5 µm at the 1.2 µm technology, for packing density (notes p. 71) Lecture 16: the spacing between the n-well and the p-well, for higher speed. The two wells abut; the notes' quantity is the spacing between diffusions
Other advantage 4 of the retrograde well
M08 §5.2		 Ends "...improvements in both the short channel threshold-roll off effect." (notes p. 72) Wolf p. 185 completes it with a second item: the punch-through characteristics of these parasitic field-region devices
Doping, depletion width and capacitance
M08 §5.2		 Reduced doping under the source and drain reduces their capacitance to the body (notes p. 72) Lecture 16: higher doping, a wider depletion region, and more capacitance with it. Both run the other way: W ∝ N−1/2, so more doping narrows the depletion region, and Cj = εSi/W rises (M02 §5.2)
The third implant of each well
M08 §6.3		 The transistor's threshold implant: phosphorus at 70 keV in the n-well, boron at 30 keV in the p-well; the 150 keV implant also serves as the channel stop (notes pp. 73–74) Lecture 16: a threshold adjustment for the field oxide, boron in both wells, and the recipe a DRAM process. The notes head it as a twin-well CMOS process; their one DRAM statement is that DRAM wells take higher doses
The n-well implants in the p. 74 sketch
M08 §6.3		 "P + P + As⁺" Wolf's Fig. 5-4(b), which the sketch redraws, has "P⁺ B⁺ As⁺"; the recipe in the notes' text and in Wolf's is all phosphorus
Implanting through an oxide to put the peak at the interface
M08 §6.4		 The threshold implant's screen oxide (Lecture 3; M03 §9.2) Lecture 16 gave that reason for the well implant, whose peak is meant to be deep. On the Gaussian a lone 600 keV phosphorus implant meets the two-junction condition (3.10), 0.73 > 0.53 µm; in the chain the 150 and 70 keV implants fill the top of the well with donors
Order of the punch-through and threshold implants
M08 §8		 Chapter 1 (p. 5): the anti-punch-through implant after the threshold implant; Chapter 5 (p. 73): the punch-through implant before it Two processes, not a contradiction: the chained implant runs from the highest energy to the lowest (Wolf p. 186). Lecture 16's punch-through implant after the source–drain formation is the halo, a third implant
ℓSDE/Xj
M10 §3.5		 ≈ 1.2 (notes p. 81) The notes' own rules, LL/2 and 0.55 LL, give 0.91; 1.2 fits the earlier 0.4 LL rule
Silicon consumed by TiSi₂
M10 §4.3		 "nearly 75 %" (notes p. 83) = 0.75 × silicide thickness (Wolf) Plummer Table 11-5: 0.90; CoSi₂ 1.03
Extension doping
M10 §1.2		 5.5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at 50 nm (notes p. 82) Lecture 17's "may be 10¹⁷" is not adopted (that is the LDD's level)
Silicide and aluminium resistivity
M10 §4.2		 TiSi₂ 13–16, CoSi₂ 14–20, Al films 2.7–3.0 µΩ·cm (Plummer) Lecture 18's 10–12, ≈ 20 and ≈ 2.4 µΩ·cm are not adopted
Organic BARC with CA resist
M11 §8.4		 "Organic BARCs cannot be used with CA resist" (notes p. 94) Wolf pp. 248–249: an acid–base incompatibility that intermixes the interface; organic BARCs with resist-matched chemistry exist for KrF and ArF
Constants in F and DOF
M11 §3.2		 F = 0.5λ/NA, DOF = 0.6λ/NA² (notes p. 98) Plummer: Rayleigh 0.61λ/NA, practical k1 0.6–0.8, DOF ±λ/(2NA²); Wolf: k1 < 0.5 by 2001
NA < 1 and the optical floor
M12 §5.1		 Optical floor 70–50 nm (notes p. 102); Wolf p. 299: no liquid medium, so NA < 1 Water immersion at 193 nm, n ≈ 1.44, NA up to 1.35 (Burnett and Kaplan 2004; Lin 2004)
PMMA tone
M12 §7		 "Negative photoresist" (Lecture 20) PMMA is positive under X-ray and electron exposure (chain scission)
EUV mirror reflectance
M12 §8.2		 Up to 70 % (notes p. 103); 80 % (Lecture 20) Wolf pp. 304–305: 68 % achieved at 13.4 nm, > 70 % needed; Plummer: above 60 %

3 · The MOSFET

ID = ZL µnCox [(VG − VT)VD − ½VD2]
(1.4)
(5.1)

What it finds: Drain current of a MOSFET in the linear region (VD < VG − VT).

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
ID
I_D, ID		drain currentAthe result of this equation
Z
Z, W		channel widthcmgiven in the problem
L
L		channel (gate) lengthcm or µmgiven in the problem
µn
mu n		electron mobility in the channelcm²/V·sgiven in the problem
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²from (3.4)
VG, VGS
V_G, VGS		gate voltageVgiven in the problem
VT
V_T, VT		threshold voltageVfrom (3.1)
VD, VDS
V_D, VDS		drain voltageVgiven in the problem
ID drain current (A) · Z channel width (cm) · L channel length (cm) · µn effective electron mobility in the channel (cm²/V·s) · Cox oxide capacitance per unit area (F/cm²) · VG, VT, VD gate, threshold and drain voltages (V); M01 writes VGS and VDS. Linear region only, valid while VD < VG − VT. The professor writes the channel width as Z; Wolf and Taur & Ning write W. The channel mobility is roughly half the bulk value, lowered further by the transverse field across the oxide. This is the notes' form; Taur & Ning carry a body-effect factor m on the last term, −(m/2)VD2, with m between 1.1 and 1.4 in a well-designed device, and the notes' form sets m = 1. Taught in M01 §4 and M05 §2.
ID,sat = µnCoxZ2L (VGS − VT)2
(1.5)

What it finds: Drain current once the channel has pinched off (saturation, VDS ≥ VGS − VT).

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
ID,sat
I_D,sat, IDsat		saturation drain currentAthe result of this equation
µn
mu n		electron mobility in the channelcm²/V·sgiven in the problem
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²from (3.4)

Z, W		channel widthcmgiven in the problem

ID,sat saturation drain current (A), the value the current holds once the channel pinches off. All other symbols as in (1.4). Saturation, valid for VDS ≥ VGS − VT: the gate-to-channel voltage at the drain end has fallen to threshold, the inversion charge there has gone to zero, and further drain voltage falls across the pinched-off region. Obtained by substituting VDS = VGS − VT into (1.4). The current rises as the square of the gate overdrive and inversely with channel length. Taught in M01 §4.
wfinal ≈ wmask + 2d   (isotropic wet etch)
M01 §7.3

What it finds: Width a wet (isotropic) etch opens: it etches sideways as far as it etches down.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
wfinal
w_final		opened width at the surface after the wet etchµmthe result of this equation
wmask
w_mask		width of the window in the resistµmgiven in the problem
d
d (etch depth)		depth etchedµmgiven in the problem
wfinal opened width at the surface (µm) · wmask width of the window in the resist (µm) · d depth etched (µm). Unnumbered in the module. Buffered oxide etch is a liquid and removes material at the same rate in every direction, so while it etches down it also etches sideways under the resist by the same distance, on each side. A 2 µm window etched through 1 µm of oxide opens to about 4 µm; a dry anisotropic etch leaves it near 2 µm. The error is tolerable for features tens of microns across and fatal at one micron, which is why directional plasma etching replaced wet etching for fine features. Taught in M01 §7.3.

4 · Threshold voltage

VT = φMS − QiCox − QdCox + 2φF
(3.1)
(2.1)

phi MS		gate-to-body work function differenceVfrom (3.3)

The professor's four-term form, with signs per §2.1. Every quantity is in centimetre units: ε0 = 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm. M05b splits Qi into the four charges of (5b.1). Cox = εrε0tox
(3.4)
(1.1)
(7.1)

What it finds: Oxide capacitance per unit area: a parallel-plate capacitor with the oxide as dielectric.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²the result of this equation
εr
epsilon r, k		relative permittivity (dielectric constant) of the filmnone3.9 for SiO₂

epsilon 0, eps0		permittivity of free spaceF/cm8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm (= 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m; use the one matching your length unit)

Cox oxide capacitance per unit area (F/cm²) · εr relative permittivity of the dielectric, 3.9 for SiO₂ (dimensionless; M01 writes it εox) · ε0 = 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm · tox oxide thickness (cm). The area is already divided out; a real gate's capacitance is Cox times its area. The product εrε0 = 3.45×10⁻¹³ F/cm is the εox of M06. Reference: 1000 Å = 10⁻⁵ cm gives 3.45×10⁻⁸ F/cm². M07 writes the same relation for any film as (7.1), C = kε0/t, with k the dielectric constant (the notes also write it ε, εox and εhigh-k) and t the physical thickness in cm; parallel plate, valid when the film is much thinner than the plate is wide. 1.5 nm of SiO₂ gives 2.30×10⁻⁶ F/cm². Taught in M03 §2, M01 §1 and M07 §1.

φMS = φM − φS
(3.3)

What it finds: Work-function difference between gate and body. Read from the graph if given; the formula is the check.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
φMS
phi MS		gate-to-body work function differenceVthe result of this equation
φM
phi M		work function of the gateVAl 4.1 V (course); n⁺ poly ≈ 4.05 V; p⁺ poly ≈ 5.17 V
φS (capital S)
phi S (work function)		work function of the silicon body = χ + Eg/2q + φF (p) or − |φF| (n). Not φsVfrom (3.3)

chi		electron affinity of siliconV4.05 V (constant) · constants

φM work function of the gate (eV, used as V; 4.1 for aluminium in this course, a MOS convention — the measured polycrystalline value is about 4.28 eV) · φS work function of the silicon body (eV, used as V). For a p-type body φS = χ + Eg/2q + φF with χ = 4.05 V and Eg/2q = 0.56 V; for an n-type body the last term is subtracted. The course reads φMS from a published graph against doping: Al on 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p-type is −0.9 V, where the formula gives −0.80 V (φS = 4.898 V). Silicon's work function spans 4.05 eV (Fermi level at Ec) to 5.17 eV (at Ev), so a degenerate n⁺ or p⁺ polysilicon gate offers about 4.05 or 5.17 eV: −0.85 and +0.27 V on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p body, −0.27 and +0.85 V on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ n body, always 1.12 V apart. Taught in M03 §1.2 and §8.

L > 2W
(2.3)

What it finds: Punch-through condition: the source and drain depletion regions must not touch.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
L
L		channel (gate) lengthcm or µmgiven in the problem
W
W (depletion)		depletion width of a junction into the bodycmfrom (2.4)
L channel length between the source and drain junctions (µm) · W depletion width of one source or drain junction into the body (µm, the same unit as L). The professor's condition (Lecture 3): the two depletion regions must not touch, and punch-through is their merging beneath the gate. Sze & Ng state it as the sum of the source and drain widths approaching L; taking W at the drain bias for both makes 2W an overestimate, so the criterion errs on the safe side. First order: it ignores the two-dimensional field under the gate. Higher body doping gives a narrower W and less punch-through, at the cost of mobility and a higher junction field. Taught in M02 §5.2.
Emax = 2(Vbi + VR)W
M02 §6.1

What it finds: Peak electric field at a one-sided junction (hot carriers, LDD).

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it

V_bi, Vbi		built-in potential of a pn junctionVfrom (2.5)

Emax peak field of a one-sided junction (V/cm) · Vbi, VR as in (2.4) (V) · W from (2.4) (cm). Unnumbered in the module. The field rises linearly across the depletion region to this maximum at the junction, so a narrower region carrying the same voltage has a higher peak: at 5 V, 4.2×10⁴ V/cm at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ and 4.3×10⁵ V/cm at 10¹⁷ cm⁻³.
