x		depth below the surfacecmgiven in the problem
Rp
R_p, Rp		projected range: depth of the implant peakcmgiven (or table)
ΔRp
delta Rp, straggle		straggle: standard deviation of the implant depthcmgiven (or table)
C(x) implanted concentration at depth x (cm⁻³) · Cp peak concentration (cm⁻³) · x depth below the surface the ions enter (cm) · Rp projected range, the depth of the peak (cm) · ΔRp straggle, the standard deviation of the depth distribution (cm). Rp and ΔRp both grow with ion energy and shrink with ion mass, and Cp = Q/(√(2π) ΔRp) for a dose Q (cm⁻²). First order: real profiles are skewed, and channelling in a crystal adds a deep tail. Threshold-adjust implants run at 25–100 keV. Taught in M03 §9.2.
Rp > ΔRp √2 ln (CpNB)
(3.10)

What it finds: Condition for an implant into bare silicon to make a second, shallow junction.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Rp
R_p, Rp		projected range: depth of the implant peakcmgiven (or table)
ΔRp
delta Rp, straggle		straggle: standard deviation of the implant depthcmgiven (or table)
Cp
C_p, Cp		peak implanted concentrationcm⁻³given in the problem
NB
N_B, NB		background doping of the opposite typecm⁻³given in the problem
Rp projected range (cm) · ΔRp straggle (cm) · Cp peak implanted concentration (cm⁻³) · NB background concentration of the opposite type (cm⁻³). The condition for a second, shallow junction when implanting into bare silicon, from (3.9) at x = 0; natural logarithm. For a peak 10⁴ times the background, 10¹⁹ over 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, the right side is 4.3ΔRp. The lecture's remedy is an oxide about Rp thick, which holds the shallow half of the profile; the textbooks' reason for that screen oxide is channelling suppression (flagged, §2.3). A threshold-adjust boron implant into p-type silicon forms no junction at all. Taught in M03 §9.2.

5.2 Wells and well implants

M08's equations, in the module's order. The notes state the well process in words and two rules of thumb, the 0.7xj of (8.5) and a 1.6:1 aspect ratio; the other relations are M08's, from Plummer, Sze & Ng and SRIM-2013, and each is marked beyond the notes. They build on the junction widths (2.4)–(2.5) and the implant profile (3.9) of §5.1, and the field threshold is (3.1). The chained recipe, ranges, straggles and diffusivities are in §16.14.

C(x, t) = Cs exp (−x24Dt),
Cs = Q√(πDt)
(8.1)
C dopant concentration (cm⁻³) at depth x (cm) after drive-in time t (s) · Cs surface concentration (cm⁻³), which falls as the dose spreads · Q dose (cm⁻²) · D = D0 exp(−EA/kT) diffusivity at the drive-in temperature (cm²/s), k = 8.617×10⁻⁵ eV/K, T in kelvin. Plummer's Table 7-3: boron D0 = 1.0 cm²/s, EA = 3.5 eV; phosphorus 4.70 cm²/s, 3.68 eV; at 1100 °C, 1.4×10⁻¹³ and 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s, and Plummer's worked example reads 1.5×10⁻¹³ for boron. A fixed dose from a layer thin against the diffusion length, none of it lost through the surface, constant intrinsic diffusivity (Plummer eq. 7.16): a half-Gaussian. The drive-in conserves Q: 0.1 µm at 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ is 10¹⁴ cm⁻², a mean of 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ over a 2 µm well. Beyond the notes, which give the time and temperature, 12–24 h above 1100 °C (p. 68). Taught in M08 §2.1.
xj = 2 √Dt ln (CsCB)
(8.2)
xj junction depth (cm) · Dt thermal budget of the drive-in (cm²) · Cs surface concentration (cm⁻³) · CB background concentration of the opposite type (cm⁻³). (8.1) with C = CB; natural logarithm. Apart from the slowly changing logarithm, the depth grows as √(Dt). Plummer's p-well: 3 µm over 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ with Cs = 4×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (from a 900 Ω/□ sheet resistance) → ln 400 = 5.99, Dt = 3.76×10⁻⁹ cm², which Plummer rounds to 3.7×10⁻⁹; at 1100 °C (D = 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s), 6.8 h (6.95 h unrounded) and Q = 4.3×10¹³ cm⁻². The same dose reaches 3.85 µm in 12 h and 5.27 µm in 24 h (× 1.37), 1 µm in 0.65 h and 2 µm in 2.9 h; any dose from 2×10¹² to 10¹⁴ cm⁻² gives 2.6–4.1 µm in 12 h. Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §2.1.
dw − xj,SD > WD + Ww
(8.3)
dw depth of the well–substrate junction (µm) · xj,SD depth of the source or drain junction (µm) · WD depletion width of the drain junction into the well, (2.4) with the well doping (µm) · Ww well-side depletion width of the well–substrate junction, (8.4) (µm). The neutral well between the two depletion regions must not vanish; first order, one-dimensional, uniform well doping. A 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ n-well on a 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ epitaxial layer, p⁺ drain at 10²⁰ cm⁻³, 5 V on both junctions: 0.880 + 0.259 = 1.14 µm, a well deeper than 1.44 µm with the drain junction at 0.3 µm; 10¹⁷ cm⁻³: 0.280 + 0.027 = 0.31 µm; 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at 3.3 V: 0.743 + 0.216 = 0.96 µm. Both junctions at the supply is the deepest depletion, so these are conservative bounds: the leakage path runs from the p⁺ source, its junction at zero bias, and closes at 0.349 + 0.259 = 0.61 µm. A constructed example on the course's equations. Taught in M08 §2.2.
Ww = √2εSiVNsubqNW(NW + Nsub)
(8.4)
Ww depletion width on the well side of the well–substrate junction (cm) · εSi = 11.8 × 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ = 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm · V = Vbi + VR the total potential across the junction (V), Vbi from (2.5) with NW and Nsub, VR the reverse bias, VDD · q = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C · NW well doping (cm⁻³) · Nsub substrate or epitaxial-layer doping (cm⁻³). Abrupt two-sided junction, depletion approximation (Sze & Ng §2.2.1): the total width divides in inverse proportion to the dopings, so when NW ≫ Nsub most of it lies in the substrate. 10¹⁶ over 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ at 5 V: Vbi = 0.635 V, Ww = 0.259 µm of a 2.84 µm total, 91% of it in the epitaxial layer. Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §2.2.
lateral spread ≈ 0.7 xj
(8.5)
Lateral spread: the distance the junction extends beneath the mask beyond the window edge (µm) · xj vertical junction depth of the diffused well (µm). The notes' rule (p. 69, after Wolf): 3–4 µm deep → 2.1–2.8 µm sideways, "2–3 µm". With it the notes give a maximum well aspect ratio, width to depth, of about 1.6:1, so wells at least 4.8–6.4 µm wide. A well is the window plus 2 × 0.7xj wide at the surface: a 2 µm window gives 3.4 µm at 1 µm deep and 4.8 µm at 2 µm. Use 0.7 and 1.6:1 as the notes do. Beyond the notes: a Gaussian drive-in under a sharp mask edge puts the lateral junction at 0.77–0.87 of the depth for surface-to-background ratios of 10²–10⁴, and on M08's reading 1.6 = 2 × 0.8 (flagged, §2.3). Taught in M08 §2.3.
VT,field = φMS − QiCFOX − QdCFOX + 2φF,
CFOX = 3.9ε0tFOX
(8.6)
VT,field threshold voltage of the field transistor, the parasitic MOSFET whose gate is any line crossing the field oxide (V) · φMS, Qi = qNi, Qd = −qNAWm and φF as in (3.1), with the signs of §2.1 · CFOX field-oxide capacitance per unit area (F/cm²) · ε0 = 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm · tFOX field-oxide thickness (cm). M03's equation unchanged; both charge terms scale with tFOX, and the field threshold must exceed the supply. Constructed example, n⁺-poly line (φMS = −(Eg/2q + φF)), Ni = 10¹¹ cm⁻², 0.5 µm of oxide (CFOX = 6.90×10⁻⁹ F/cm²): 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ → −0.907 − 2.32 + 6.98 + 0.695 = 4.45 V; 5×10¹⁵ → 2.26 V; 5×10¹⁶ → 14.0 V. Under 1 µm: 9.1, 4.7 and 28 V; under 0.3 µm: 2.6, 1.3 and 8.3 V. The same 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ under a 10 nm gate oxide gives −0.1 V. Taught in M08 §3.1.
m = CSiCSiO₂
(8.7)
m segregation coefficient at the Si/SiO₂ interface (dimensionless) · CSi, CSiO₂ dopant concentrations on the silicon and the oxide side of the interface (cm⁻³). Plummer §7.5.4: about 0.3 for boron, about 10 for phosphorus, arsenic and antimony, varying with temperature and, for boron, with the moisture in the oxidising ambient. A growing oxide sweeps through the silicon: boron is taken into the oxide and the silicon beneath is depleted, to about a quarter of its level at the interface in Plummer's simulation, while phosphorus piles up ahead of the interface, to about 1.4 times. The reason LOCOS lowers the p-well's field threshold (the notes: boron "segregates into the oxide", p. 69). Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §3.2.
C(x, y) =
Cvert(x) · ½ erfc (y√2 ΔR⊥)
(8.8)
C(x, y) implanted concentration (cm⁻³) at depth x and lateral distance y from the mask edge, positive under the mask (cm) · Cvert(x) the profile in depth far inside the window, (3.9) · ΔR⊥ lateral straggle, the standard deviation of the ions' sideways rest positions (cm) · erfc the complementary error function. Half the window value at the edge itself. Plummer's point-response form (eq. 8.5) summed over a straight edge; it neglects ions scattered back out of the mask's sidewall. SRIM-2013 ΔR⊥: boron 0.18 µm at 500 keV and 0.15 µm at 300 keV, phosphorus 0.14 µm at 600 keV. A 3×10¹³ cm⁻² boron implant at 500 keV reaches 0.53 µm under the mask at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ and 0.65 µm at 10¹⁴ cm⁻³, three to four ΔR⊥. The notes' "lateral straggle for 500 keV is ~0.7 µm" (p. 70, Wolf's figure) is the course value; as a standard deviation it is about four times SRIM's (flagged, §2.3). Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §4.2.
σ2 = ΔRp2 + 2Dt,
C(x) = Cp exp [−(x − Rp)22σ2],
Cp = Q√(2π) σ
(8.9)
σ standard deviation of the annealed profile (cm) · ΔRp as-implanted straggle (cm) · D diffusivity at the anneal temperature (cm²/s) · t anneal time (s) · Cp peak concentration (cm⁻³) · Q dose (cm⁻²) · Rp projected range (cm). A Gaussian of straggle ΔRp has the shape of a delta function diffused for a thermal budget ΔRp2/2, so an anneal adds its own Dt (Plummer eq. 8.12). The centre stays at Rp; the peak falls as ΔRp/σ. Constant diffusivity, peak far from the surface compared with σ; damage-enhanced diffusion early in an anneal adds to Dt (M09). Phosphorus at 600 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻² (Rp 0.727 µm, ΔRp 0.144 µm), 30 min at 1000 °C (D = 1.27×10⁻¹⁴ cm²/s): √(2Dt) = 0.068 µm, σ = 0.159 µm, peak 8.3 → 7.5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, and the deep-side junction at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ moves from 1.26 to 1.31 µm; 5 min: 0.147 µm, × 0.98; 30 min at 1100 °C (1.46×10⁻¹³ cm²/s): 0.271 µm, × 0.53, junction 1.67 µm. Plummer's damage anneal: about 10 s at 1000 °C or 30 min at 800 °C. Beyond the notes, which give a short anneal at a relatively low temperature (p. 70). Taught in M08 §4.3.
αnpn + αpnp ≥ 1
⇔  βnpnβpnp ≥ 1
(8.10)
α common-base current gain of each parasitic transistor, the fraction of its emitter current that reaches its collector (dimensionless) · β = α/(1 − α), its common-emitter gain (dimensionless). The thyristor switching condition (Sze & Ng §11.2) with no shunting resistance, each base open to its emitter, applied to the vertical pnp (p⁺ source, n-well, p-type substrate) and the lateral npn (n⁺ source, p-well and substrate, n-well) of bulk CMOS. The well and substrate resistances carry part of each base current around the emitter junction, so low resistances raise the gain product needed; first order. Trigger: a transient current that drops about 0.7 V across Rwell or Rsub (Plummer p. 467); the latched state persists until the supply is removed. Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §5.1.
|E| = kTq · 1N dNdx
(8.11)
E built-in field of a non-uniformly doped region (V/cm) · kT/q = 0.0259 V at 300 K · N(x) the doping (cm⁻³) at position x (cm). Equilibrium, non-degenerate doping (Sze & Ng p. 248, eqs. 14–15). In n-type material the field points from the heavier toward the lighter doping and pushes holes that way. In a retrograde n-well, whose donors rise with depth toward the buried peak, the field between the p⁺ emitter and the peak points up and pushes the injected holes back toward the emitter, lowering the vertical pnp's gain: the notes' "retarded electric field" (pp. 71–72). One straggle above the 600 keV peak, where the doping changes by a factor e in about 0.14 µm, 0.0259 V ÷ 1.44×10⁻⁵ cm ≈ 1.8 kV/cm. A diffused well, highest at the surface, has the gradient the other way, as in Sze & Ng's drift transistor. Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §5.2.
Cj = εSiW
(8.12)

What it finds: Junction capacitance: a parallel-plate capacitor whose plate gap is the depletion width.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Cj
C_j, Cj		junction capacitance per cm²F/cm²the result of this equation
εs, εsi
epsilon s, epsilon si, eps Si		permittivity of silicon, 11.8 × ε0F/cm1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm (constant) · constants
W
W (depletion)		depletion width of a junction into the bodycmfrom (2.4)
Cj junction capacitance per unit area (F/cm²) · εSi = 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm · W depletion width (cm), from (2.4) for a one-sided junction. A parallel-plate capacitor whose plate separation is the depletion width; depletion approximation. Since W ∝ N−1/2, lighter doping beneath a junction gives a smaller capacitance, the notes' fifth advantage of the retrograde well. n⁺ (10²⁰ cm⁻³) over p at 0 V: 10¹⁶ cm⁻³, 0.349 µm, 29.9 nF/cm²; 10¹⁷, 0.114 µm, 91.7 nF/cm²; 10¹⁸, 0.037 µm, 282 nF/cm²; 3.3 V on 10¹⁶: 0.743 µm, 14.0 nF/cm². The chained p-well's minimum, 3.1×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at 0.25 µm: at 3×10¹⁶, 0.204 µm and 51 nF/cm², 0.56 of the 10¹⁷ value. A constructed example. Taught in M08 §5.2, and in the M02 §5.2 box.
tresist ≥ Rp,resist + 5 ΔRp,resist
(8.13)
tresist minimum resist thickness (µm) · Rp,resist, ΔRp,resist projected range and straggle of the ion in the resist (µm), which exceed their values in silicon. Five straggles beyond the range, the Gaussian has fallen to e−12.5 ≈ 4×10⁻⁶ of its peak. A rule of thumb, used in Coherent's SRIM-2013 charts for AZ-111 resist; high doses shrink the resist and may need more. Phosphorus at 600 keV: 1.32 + 5 × 0.146 = 2.05 µm, the ion ranging 1.8 times as far as in silicon; boron at 300 keV, 1.40 + 5 × 0.136 = 2.08 µm; at 500 keV, 2.06 + 5 × 0.157 = 2.84 µm. The notes: 2.0–2.5 µm (pp. 72, 74); Plummer: "thick (2-micron)" resist. Beyond the notes. Taught in M08 §6.1.

5.3 Channel profiles

NA > 8εSi(Vbi + VR)qL2
(9.1)
NA minimum uniform body doping (cm⁻³) · εSi = 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm · Vbi from (2.5) (V) · VR drain reverse bias (V) · q = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C · L channel length (cm). L > 2W (2.3) with W from (2.4); one-sided abrupt junction; iterate for Vbi. A first-order rule. Taught in M09 §1.1.
|x − Rp| = ΔRp √(2 ln r)
(9.2)
Distance from the peak of a Gaussian implant (3.9) at which the concentration is Cp/r, in the unit of ΔRp. One decade 2.15 ΔRp, two decades 3.03 ΔRp. As implanted; no skewness, channelling tail or diffusion. SRIM ranges for B, P, As, In and Sb: M09 Table 1. Taught in M09 §2.1.
ΔXj2 ∝ N Rp exp (+1.4 eVkT)
(9.3)
ΔXj junction deepening by transient enhanced diffusion · N ≈ implanted dose (cm⁻²) · Rp projected range · k = 8.617×10⁻⁵ eV/K · T temperature at which the extended defects dissolve (K). Wolf Vol. 4 eq. 5.9; a proportionality, for ratios. The positive exponent is the "negative activation energy": a lower dissolution temperature gives more TED. Taught in M09 §4.3.
VT = φMS − QiCox + 2φF + qNA(Wdm − xs)Cox
(9.4)
Wdm = (4εSiφFqNA + xs2)1/2
(9.5)
n-channel threshold for an ideal step: undoped surface layer of thickness xs (cm) over acceptors NA (cm⁻³) · Wdm maximum gate depletion width (cm) · φF of the heavy layer (V) · Cox (F/cm²); other symbols as in M03. Taur & Ning 3/E (6.120)–(6.121), in the professor's notation; valid while Wdm > xs. For xs much larger than the uniform Wdm the depletion term tends to εSi(2φF/xs)/Cox, half the uniform value at equal depth (ground plane). Taught in M09 §5.1.

5.4 Source and drain

ρs = ρt  R = ρs LW
(10.1)
ρs sheet resistance (Ω/□) · ρ resistivity (Ω·cm) · t layer thickness or junction depth (cm) · R resistance (Ω) · L/W number of squares, length along the current over width (same unit). Uniform thin layer. Taur & Ning eqs. (2.40)–(2.41). Taught in M10 §2.1.
Rs = Rco + RSDE + Rsp + Rac
(10.2)
Series resistance of one source or drain (Ω): contact, extension (Rsh without an extension), spreading, accumulation layer. Notes pp. 78–79, Wolf eqs. 5.1–5.2. Not the sheet resistance ρs. Taught in M10 §3.1.
RsatW = VDD tox0.47 vsat εox (VDD − VT) ≈ tox0.376 vsat εox
(10.3)
Rsat = VDD/IDsat; RsatW in Ω·cm · tox (cm) · vsat ≈ 10⁷ cm/s · εox = 3.45×10⁻¹³ F/cm. The ≈ form assumes VT = 0.2 VDD (0.47 × 0.8 = 0.376). Design goal: source + drain series resistance cuts IDsat by ≤ 10 % (≈ 10 % of Rsat costs ≈ 8 %). Notes p. 79, Wolf eq. 5.4. Taught in M10 §3.2.
Rsp ≈ 2ρπW ln (β XjXc)
(10.4)
Spreading resistance (Ω) · ρ extension resistivity (Ω·cm) · W width (cm) · Xj junction depth over Xc inversion/accumulation layer thickness (ratio ≫ 1) · β 0.37–0.9 (Wolf 0.58; Taur & Ning 0.75). Abrupt, uniform extension. Notes p. 81, Wolf eq. 5.5. Taught in M10 §3.4.
RSDE ≈ ρ ℓSDEW Xj = ρs ℓSDEW
(10.5)
Extension resistance (Ω) · ℓSDE ≈ LL/2 · Xj ≈ 0.55 LL (notes p. 81; these give ℓSDE/Xj = 0.91, the notes write 1.2). Taught in M10 §3.5.
(Rsp + RSDE) W ≈ ρ ℓSDEXj [1 + 2Xjπ ℓSDE ln (β XjXc)]
(10.6)
Ω·cm. ρℓSDE/Xj = ρsℓSDE. Bracket 2.09 at 250 nm, ≈ 1.48 at 50 nm (Wolf). Allowed extension sheet resistance ≈ 650 Ω/□ at 250 nm (notes), ≈ 900 Ω/□ at 50 nm (Wolf); p-channel ≈ 20 % higher. Notes p. 81, Wolf eq. 5.7. Taught in M10 §3.5.
RcoW ≥ ρc2 LL
(10.7)
Ω·cm · ρc specific contact resistivity, silicide–silicon (Ω·cm²) · LL drawn gate length (cm). Targets 10⁻⁷ Ω·cm² (0.25 µm), 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm² (50 nm); interface doping > 10²⁰ cm⁻³ for a 0.5 eV barrier. Notes p. 83, Wolf eq. 5.8. Taught in M10 §4.1.
Imax ∝ V3/2
(10.8)
Child's law: largest ion current extractable at extraction voltage V; for ratios. Why sub-keV implants are slow (1–2 mA against 10–25 mA above 10 keV). Wolf p. 206. Taught in M10 §6.2.
Cov ≈ εoxW (lovtox + 7)
(10.9)
Overlap capacitance of one side at Vgs = 0 (F) · εox = 3.45×10⁻¹³ F/cm · W, lov, tox in cm. The 7 is the two fringing terms for a gate ≈ 40 tox tall and a junction ≈ 20 tox deep; the inner-fringe part (≈ 4.6) exists only below threshold, so with the channel on use + 2.3. Taur & Ning eqs. (8.24)–(8.26). Taught in M10 §7.1.

6 · Scaling and the gate dielectric

LEtox ≈ 45
(2.2)

What it finds: Scaling rule of thumb: gate length is about 45 times the oxide thickness.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
LE
L_E		gate lengthsame unit as toxgiven in the problem
tox
t_ox, tox		oxide thicknesscmgiven. Convert: 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm, 1 nm = 10⁻⁷ cm
LE gate length (any length unit) · tox gate oxide thickness (the same unit). Dimensionless. A design rule, not a physical law: it records the ratio at which gate control over the channel stayed adequate against the drain. The notes' points: 1 µm → 200 Å, 0.18 µm → 40 Å, below 0.1 µm → about 20 Å, which sit on ratios of 50, 45 and 50. The 0.18 µm point divides the generation name, not the physical gate length (flagged, §2.3). Taught in M02 §4.
Eox = Vtox
(5.2)

What it finds: Oxide field from the voltage across it; compare with 8–10 MV/cm breakdown.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Eox
E_ox, Eox		electric field in the oxideV/cmthe result of this equation
V
V		voltage across the oxideVgiven in the problem
tox
t_ox, tox		oxide thicknesscmgiven. Convert: 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm, 1 nm = 10⁻⁷ cm
Eox oxide electric field (V/cm) · V voltage across the oxide (V) · tox oxide thickness (cm). Uniform-field approximation for a parallel-plate structure. Convert first: 5 nm = 50 Å = 5×10⁻⁷ cm, and 1 MV/cm = 10⁶ V/cm. Compare against 8–10 MV/cm, the breakdown field of a high-quality dry oxide, and the notes' requirement that it exceed 8 MV/cm; the notes also give a 20-year life below 8 MV/cm and a typical logic and memory field in the 5 MV/cm range. The two uses of 8 MV/cm cannot both hold (flagged, §2.3): time to breakdown falls by roughly a decade per 0.3–0.5 MV/cm. Rearranged, tox ≥ V/Eox gives the thinnest oxide a supply permits at a chosen service field. Taught in M05 §4.2.

The equivalent oxide thickness, which M02 §4 states without a number, is (7.3) for a single high-k film and (7.6) for a stack, in §11 with the rest of the high-k gate dielectric.

7 · Bonds, structure and oxidation

I = 1 − exp (− (XA − XB)24)
(5a.1)

What it finds: Fraction of ionic character of a bond from the electronegativity difference.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
I
I (ionic)		fractional ionic character of the bond: 0 covalent, → 1 ionicnonethe result of this equation
XA, XB
X_A, X_B		Pauling electronegativities of the two atomsnonegiven (values in §16.3)
I fractional ionic character of the A–B bond, dimensionless: 0 for a purely covalent bond, approaching 1 for a fully ionic one · XA, XB Pauling electronegativities of the two atoms, dimensionless (values in §16.3). An empirical relation: it estimates the polarity of a bond, not a measured charge. Si–O: 3.44 − 1.90 = 1.54, 1.54²/4 = 0.593, I = 1 − e−0.593 = 0.45 (51% on Pauling's 1960 values). Si–N 28%, Si–F 66%, Si–H 2%. The notes' Si(+4) and O²⁻ are formal oxidation states, bookkeeping for a bond about half ionic. Taught in M05a §1.
N(100) = 2a2 ,   N(111) = 4√3 a2
(5a.2)

What it finds: Silicon atoms per cm² on a crystal plane.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
N(100)
N(100)		silicon atoms per cm² on a (100) planecm⁻²the result of this equation
a
a (lattice)		lattice constant of siliconcm5.431×10⁻⁸ cm (5.431 Å)
N number of silicon atoms per unit area of one atomic plane (cm⁻²) · a lattice constant of silicon, 5.431 Å = 5.431×10⁻⁸ cm; enter it in centimetres so that N comes out in cm⁻². With a² = 2.950×10⁻¹⁵ cm², N(100) = 6.8×10¹⁴ cm⁻² and N(111) = 7.8×10¹⁴ cm⁻². Ideal planes. An ideal (100) surface atom has two outward bonds and a (111) atom one, so the bond densities are 1.36×10¹⁵ and 7.8×10¹⁴ cm⁻² — the order reversed. The notes' square grid draws one outward bond per atom whatever the plane. Taught in M05a §2.
xSi = nSiO₂nSi xox = 0.46 xox

nSi
n_Si		silicon atoms per cm³ of crystalcm⁻³5.00×10²² cm⁻³ (constant) · constants
xSi thickness of silicon consumed · xox thickness of oxide grown, in the same length unit · nSiO₂ SiO₂ units per unit volume of thermal oxide, 2.28×10²² cm⁻³ · nSi atoms per unit volume of crystalline silicon, 5.00×10²² cm⁻³. The ratio 2.28/5.00 = 0.456 is the 0.46 of Lectures 8 and 9. Of the finished film, 0.46 lies below the original silicon surface and 0.54 above it. For growth on a flat surface. Texts quote 0.44–0.46 (Plummer 0.45), the spread of the oxide density; Wolf's 2.20 g/cm³ gives 0.44. Taught in M05a §4; stated in M01 §7.1.

8 · Oxide and interface charge

Qi = Qf + Qit + Qot + Qm
(5b.1)

What it finds: The total interface charge in VT is the sum of the four oxide charges.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Qi
Q_i, Qi (total)		total interface charge used in VTC/cm²the result of this equation
Qf
Q_f, Qf		fixed oxide charge, positive, within about 2 nm of the interface (counted as Nf)C/cm²given in the problem
Qit
Q_it, Qit		interface trapped charge (counted as Nit or Dit)C/cm²given in the problem

Q_ot, Qot		oxide trapped chargeC/cm²given in the problem

Q_m, Qm		mobile ionic charge (Na⁺, K⁺)C/cm²given in the problem
Qi total interface charge per unit area in the threshold equation (C/cm²) · Qf fixed oxide charge, counted as Nf (cm⁻²): positive, within about 2 nm of the interface, in the transition region · Qit interface trapped charge, counted as Nit (cm⁻²) or Dit (cm⁻² eV⁻¹): on the interface, from dangling bonds · Qot oxide trapped charge, Not (cm⁻²): in the bulk, either sign · Qm mobile ionic charge, Nm (cm⁻²): Na⁺ and K⁺, anywhere in the oxide, drifting under bias and temperature. All in C/cm², each positive for positive charge; Q = qN. Unweighted only for charge at the interface (see (5b.2)). The notes: Qit and Qot are the ongoing reliability problem; their sentence that Qm and Qf are "not well controlled" reverses Wolf's "not only well controlled" (flagged, §2.3). Taught in M05b §1.
ΔVT = − QoxCox
(5b.2)

What it finds: Threshold shift caused by charge in the oxide.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
ΔVT
delta VT, dVT		threshold-voltage shiftVthe result of this equation
Qox
Q_ox, Qox		effective oxide charge per cm²C/cm²from (5b.1)
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²from (3.4)
ΔVT threshold voltage shift (V) · Qox effective oxide charge per unit area (C/cm²) · Cox = εrε0/tox (F/cm²), with εr = 3.9, ε0 = 8.85×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm and tox in cm. Positive oxide charge gives a negative shift. The notes write it on p. 27. "Effective" means weighted by position: charge at distance x from the gate in an oxide of thickness tox contributes only x/tox of the shift it would produce at the silicon interface. The bare sum of (5b.1) is exact for Qit, close for Qf, an upper bound for Qot and Qm. Taught in M05b §3.3.
Nit = f · N(100)

Unnumbered in the module; one electronic charge per state. The notes' worked case (p. 25): f = 10⁻³ gives 6.8×10¹¹ cm⁻², 1.09×10⁻⁷ C/cm², and through 20 nm (Cox = 1.73×10⁻⁷ F/cm²) |ΔVT| = 0.63 V: an interface 99.9% perfect is not good enough. The notes apply the fraction to atoms; applied to the 1.36×10¹⁵ outward bonds it would double the result. The lecture's target of one in 10⁴ gives 0.063 V; after a hydrogen anneal the density can be as low as 10¹⁰ cm⁻². Taught in M05b §3.3.
Dit = 1q dQitdE
(5b.3)

What it finds: Interface-trap density per unit energy.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Dit
D_it, Dit		interface-trap density per unit energycm⁻² eV⁻¹the result of this equation
q
q		electron chargeC1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C (constant) · constants
Qit, E
Q_it, E		interface trapped charge; trap energy in the gapC/cm², eVgiven in the problem
Dit interface-trap density per unit area per unit energy (cm⁻² eV⁻¹) · q = 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C · Qit interface trapped charge (C/cm²) · E energy of the trap level in the gap (eV). Integrating Dit over an energy range gives Nit in cm⁻². Interface-trap levels are so closely spaced that they form a continuum over the gap, which is why traps are counted per unit energy; measured Dit is roughly constant near midgap and rises toward both band edges. Taught in M05b §3.5.
C = Cox(CD + Cit)Cox + CD + Cit ,   Cit = q2Dit
(5b.4)

What it finds: Measured low-frequency gate capacitance with interface traps.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it

C (measured)		measured low-frequency gate capacitanceF/cm²the result of this equation
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²from (3.4)
CD
C_D, CD		depletion capacitance of the siliconF/cm²given in the problem
Cit
C_it, Cit		interface-trap capacitance = q²Dit (Dit per J) or q·Dit (per eV)F/cm²found with this equation
Dit
D_it, Dit		interface-trap density per unit energycm⁻² eV⁻¹from (5b.3)

q		electron chargeC1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C (constant) · constants
C measured gate capacitance per unit area, low-frequency limit (F/cm²) · Cox oxide capacitance (F/cm²) · CD depletion capacitance of the silicon (F/cm²) · Cit interface-trap capacitance (F/cm²) · Dit from (5b.3). With Dit per joule, q²Dit is in F/cm²; with Dit in the usual cm⁻² eV⁻¹, Cit = q × Dit numerically, q in coulombs. Cit is in parallel with CD, and the pair in series with Cox, so no trap density raises C above Cox; traps stretch the C–V curve along the voltage axis. At high frequency the traps cannot follow and the Cit term drops out, yet the curve still stretches, because the traps follow the slow d.c. sweep. Taught in M05b §3.7.
H2 → 2H,   ≡Si• + H → ≡Si–H
(5b.5)
≡Si• a trivalent silicon: three bonds (≡) and one unpaired electron (•) · H atomic hydrogen · ≡Si–H the passivated site. Molecular hydrogen diffuses through the oxide, dissociates at the interface, and the atom bonds to the dangling orbital, which leaves no level in the gap. Both reactions are reversible; the second runs backward when a hot carrier breaks the Si–H bond (mean 3.30 eV; about 2.83 eV for H on an interfacial Pb site on (111)), which restores the trap. Fixed charge is not affected. Conditions in §16.5. Taught in M05b §4.1.

9 · Conduction, breakdown and the thickness limit

JFN = q2Eox216π2ℏφox exp (− 4(2qm*)1/2φox3/23ℏEox )

q

JFN tunnelling current density (A/m², converted afterwards) · q = 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C · Eox oxide field (V/m) · ℏ = 1.055×10⁻³⁴ J·s · φox Si–SiO₂ barrier in volts, 3.1 V, so that qφox is the 3.1 eV barrier energy · m* electron effective mass in the oxide (kg), commonly 0.5m0. Written in SI and evaluated in SI: 1 A/m² = 10⁻⁴ A/cm². Fed V/cm the exponent is inflated by exactly 100 — at 10 MV/cm about −26 becomes −2636 — and the equation returns zero. Check: 10 MV/cm = 1×10⁹ V/m with m* = 0.5m0 gives 1.8 A/m² = 1.8×10⁻⁴ A/cm². Ignores finite temperature and image-force lowering. Applies only while the tunnelling distance φox/Eox is shorter than the oxide, which at 8 MV/cm is 3.9 nm; otherwise the transport is direct tunnelling, which has no closed form here. log(J/Eox2) against 1/Eox is a straight line for F–N, and its slope fixes m*1/2φox3/2, not the barrier alone. Measured at 8 MV/cm: about 5×10⁻⁷ A/cm² (Wolf); (6.1) gives 1.6×10⁻⁷. Taught in M06 §1.2.
Jmax = IoffW L
(6.2)

What it finds: Largest tolerable gate leakage: gate tunnelling current equal to the off-state leakage.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it

Jmax largest tolerable gate tunnelling current density (A/µm² as computed; multiply by 10⁸ µm²/cm² for A/cm²) · Ioff off-state source-to-drain leakage current (A) · W gate width (µm) · L gate length (µm). The notes' criterion: gate tunnelling current equal to the off-state subthreshold leakage. Leakage is specified per unit width, Ioff/W in A/µm, so the width cancels: Jmax = (Ioff/W)/L. Gate leakage scales with area and subthreshold leakage with width, so the ceiling carries a factor 1/L. The notes' 1 A/cm² is 10 nA/µm at L = 1 µm: 10⁻⁸ A over 1 µm² = 10⁻⁸ A/µm², and since 1 cm² = 10⁸ µm², 1 A/cm². The factor is 10⁸, not 10⁴. Taught in M06 §2.
Vg − VFB = Vpoly + Vox + φs
(6.9)

What it finds: How the gate voltage splits between poly depletion, oxide and silicon.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Vg
V_g, Vg		gate voltageVgiven in the problem
VFB
V_FB, VFB		flat-band voltage, = φMS with no oxide chargeVfrom (3.3)
Vpoly
V_poly, Vpoly		voltage across the depleted layer in the poly gateVfrom (6.10)

V_ox, Vox		voltage across the oxide = Qg/CoxVfrom (6.9)

phi s (surface)		surface potential: total band bending at the surface; 2φF at inversion. Not φSVfrom (1.3)
Vg gate voltage (V) · VFB flat-band voltage (V), equal to φMS when the oxide holds no charge: −0.85 to −0.97 V for an n⁺ polysilicon gate on 10¹⁵–10¹⁷ cm⁻³ p-type silicon · Vpoly voltage across the depleted layer in the gate, its upward band bending (V), from (6.10) · Vox = Qg/Cox, voltage across the oxide (V) · φs surface potential of the silicon, its band bending qφs (V). The gate voltage beyond flat band divides into three drops once the gate depletes; with an ideal gate Vpoly = 0 and two remain. No current is involved: each drop is the voltage of a charged capacitor. In strong inversion φs is nearly pinned, so Vpoly comes almost entirely out of Vox. For 20 Å on 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ under an 8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ gate at Vg − VFB = 2.02 V, Vpoly is 0.093 V, taken as 88 mV from the oxide and 5 mV from the surface potential, and the inversion charge falls 10%. It is Taur & Ning's Eq. (4.50), written there with ψp for Vpoly and ψs for φs. Lecture 11 wrote the left side as VG, taking φMS ≈ 0, which for this pair is about 1 V off (flagged, §2.3). Taught in M06 §6.1.
1Ctot = 1Cpolydep + 1Cox
(6.3)

What it finds: Total gate capacitance: poly depletion in series with the oxide.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
Ctot
C_tot, Ctot		total gate capacitance per cm²F/cm²the result of this equation
Cpolydep
C_polydep, Cpolydep		capacitance of the poly depletion layer per cm²F/cm²from (6.4)
Cox
C_ox, Cox		oxide capacitance per cm²F/cm²from (3.4)
Ctot total gate capacitance per unit area (F/cm²) · Cpolydep capacitance of the depletion layer in the polysilicon gate (F/cm²), the notes' C1 · Cox = εox/tox (F/cm²), the notes' C2, with εox = 3.45×10⁻¹³ F/cm. Capacitors in series: the reciprocals add, so the smaller capacitor largely sets the total, which is always below it. The substrate depletion capacitance is in the same chain and is omitted. The notes' first-order statement: Cox is much smaller than Cpolydep, and so dominates, until the oxide is thinner than about 10 nm; as the oxide thins Cox rises while Cpolydep remains constant at fixed poly doping, so below about 10 nm Cpolydep begins to impact the total (Wolf: below about 10 nm and at gate voltages of 2.5 V or lower). Lecture 11's 2–4 nm is where the effect is large, 18% of the capacitance at 20 Å. The oxide remains the smaller capacitor in every case worked — Cpolydep/Cox = 28 at 100 Å, 4.2 at 15 Å, 4.5 in the 20 Å problem — and Cpolydep becomes the smaller only when Wpd exceeds (εsi/εox)tox ≈ 3tox (for 20 Å at 1 V, a gate doped below about 1.8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³). Lecture 11 said the reverse (flagged, §2.3). Poly depletion raises the equivalent oxide thickness, lowers the inversion charge, and lowers ID and speed; heavier poly doping narrows the depleted layer. Refinement, flagged in §2.3: Cpolydep is constant only at fixed gate charge too, and as oxide fields rose it fell, which strengthens the notes' conclusion. Taught in M06 §6.2.

Active doping reaches about 10²⁰ cm⁻³ for n-type poly and only the mid-to-upper 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ for p-type, which is why p⁺ gates suffer worse: 5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at 5 MV/cm gives Wpd = 2.16 nm and tpd = 7.1 Å. All lengths in centimetres. Depletion approximation with uniform active doping: the field rises linearly across the layer and the potential quadratically, the depletion relation of M03 §4 applied to the gate. Because Wpd is proportional to Qg, Vpoly grows as the square of the gate charge and so of the oxide field; at a fixed oxide voltage it rises as 1/tox2, and at fixed charge it varies as 1/Np. Work the width first, as Lecture 11 did, and keep it unrounded: the square makes rounding matter. 1 V on 20 Å, 8×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ → 13.5 Å, 0.111 V (13 Å gives 0.104 V, the class's 0.1035 V); 10²⁰ cm⁻³ at 5 MV/cm → 0.089 V; 1 V on 15 Å at 10²⁰ cm⁻³ (6.7 MV/cm) → 0.158 V; 5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at 5 MV/cm → 0.178 V. Inverted, holding Vpoly to 50 mV at 5 MV/cm needs Np ≥ 1.8×10²⁰ cm⁻³, beyond what polysilicon can hold active; a metal gate, whose 10²²–10²³ conduction electrons per cm³ screen the charge within about an ångström, makes Vpoly effectively zero. Taur & Ning write it as Qg = √(2εsiqNpψp). Taught in M06 §6.2.

10 · Growing thin oxides and changing their chemistry

tox2 + A tox = B(t + τ)
(6.5)

tox oxide thickness (µm) · t oxidation time (hr) · B parabolic rate constant (µm²/hr), set by oxidant diffusion through the oxide · B/A linear rate constant (µm/hr), set by the reaction at the interface · A (µm) · τ (hr) shift of the time origin for an oxide of thickness ti present at the start, τ = (ti2 + Ati)/B. Both constants depend on temperature (Arrhenius) and ambient, and doping affects them; orientation affects only B/A. Thin film: tox ≈ (B/A)(t + τ), linear, reaction-limited. Thick film: tox2 ≈ B(t + τ), parabolic, diffusion-limited. In the notes' words the model predicts films thicker than 350 Å well; below 200 Å, for dry oxidation, is its anomalous region, where dry growth runs faster than predicted. Deal & Grove, J. Appl. Phys. 36, 3770 (1965). Taught in M06 §8.1.
tox = a(tg + τ)b
(6.6)

Reisman and Nicollian (1987), for dry O₂; it fits all published dry-O₂ data down to essentially zero thickness, so on this description there is no anomalous regime. Empirical in form, but with a physical basis the authors attached to it: the interface reaction controls oxidation at all thicknesses, and viscous flow of the oxide accommodates the volume expansion at the interface, which they used to explain how a and b vary with temperature and pressure. b runs from 0.25 to 1.0. Wolf's constants for 1 atm dry O₂: 800 °C, a = 0.302 nm, b = 0.704, τ = 13.1 min; 1000 °C, a = 3.02 nm, b = 0.701, τ = 1.26 min. Taught in M06 §8.1.

dtoxdt = B2tox + A + C exp (− toxL2 )

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it

C, L2 (Massoud)		enhancement amplitude C = C0exp(−EA/kT), decay length L2µm/hr; nmC0 ≈ 3.6×10⁸ µm/hr, EA ≈ 2.35 eV, L2 ≈ 7 nm
tox oxide thickness (µm) · t time (hr) · A, B the Deal–Grove constants of (6.5); the first term is (6.5) differentiated · C = C0exp(−EA/kT), with C0 ≈ 3.6×10⁸ µm/hr and EA ≈ 2.35 eV (k in eV/K) · L2 ≈ 7 nm, the decay length of the enhancement. Massoud's correction: dry oxidation of lightly doped substrates over 800–1000 °C, independent of surface orientation; the second term is negligible once tox ≫ L2. It is the thin-oxide model implemented in SUPREM III and IV, and generally in the process simulators built on Deal–Grove, because it keeps A and B and so inherits the other oxidation physics expressed through them; Reisman's is not. Taught in M06 §8.1.
WF₆ + 3H₂ → W + 6HF
2WF₆ + 3SiH₄ → 2W + 3SiF₄ + 6H₂
(6.11)
Hydrogen reduction (top) and silane reduction (bottom) of tungsten hexafluoride, WF₆, in the low-pressure chemical vapour deposition of tungsten: a hot-wall reactor at 250–500 °C and 0.1–2 Torr. For tungsten silicide the same two gases are used with a high SiH₄:WF₆ ratio, so that WSi₂ deposits instead of W. Every WF₆ brings six fluorine atoms; the gaseous products, HF and SiF₄, carry most of them away. CVD tungsten retains fluorine from its source, often 1 atomic percent or more (Plummer) — at 1 atomic percent about one fluorine kept for every six hundred the reacting WF₆ brought in — and CVD WSix holds fluorine too. In a polycide gate the WSix lies directly on the polysilicon, and later anneals drive the retained fluorine down through it to the Si/SiO₂ interface, where it replaces Si–H with Si–F: the second of the notes' two routes to a fluorinated oxide. The reaction is a reduction, not a thermal decomposition; Lecture 10's WF₆ → W + 3F₂ at 700–800 °C would cost +1632 kJ/mol at 25 °C (flagged, §2.3). Plummer, Deal & Griffin Eqs. (9.35)–(9.36). Taught in M06 §9.4.

11 · High-k gate dielectrics

The capacitance per unit area of any film, C = kε0/t, is (7.1), listed with (3.4) in §4. The notes write the dielectric constant k, ε, εox and εhigh-k for the same dimensionless ratio; this section writes k, and ε0 only for the permittivity of free space. Material values are in §16.11.

k = n2 + klat,    klat ∝ N Z*2μ ωTO2
(7.2)
k static dielectric constant (dimensionless) · n optical refractive index (dimensionless); n² is the electronic part · klat the ionic, or lattice, part (dimensionless) · N number of ions per unit volume · Z* transverse, or Born, effective charge, in units of the electron charge · μ reduced mass of the cation–anion pair · ωTO angular frequency of the transverse optical phonon. A proportionality, written to show the dependences, so it fixes no unit system; Robertson (2004, eq. 11) gives the full expression. With ωTO2 = f/μ, f the restoring force constant (N/m), the denominator is f and the ionic masses cancel. Applies well below the infrared, which includes every frequency a transistor operates at. Beyond the notes. SiO₂: n ≈ 1.46, so about 2.1 of its 3.9 is electronic and 1.8 ionic; HfO₂: roughly 4 electronic and 20 ionic of about 24. A large k needs a large Z* (5.85 for Hf in cubic HfO₂, against a nominal +4) and a soft polar vibration. The electronic structure that gives a large k gives a small gap — in HfO₂, ZrO₂ and Ta₂O₅ the conduction band is formed from the metal's empty d orbitals — so k and Eg run inversely. Taught in M07 §2.
tox,eq = thigh-k (εoxεhigh-k) = thigh-k (3.9k)
(7.3)

What it finds: Equivalent oxide thickness of a high-k film.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
tox,eq
t_ox,eq, EOT		equivalent oxide thickness: SiO₂ thickness with the same capacitancenm or Åthe result of this equation
thigh-k
t_high-k		physical thickness of the high-k filmnm or Ågiven in the problem
k, εhigh-k
k (dielectric constant)		dielectric constant of the high-k film (SiO₂ is 3.9)nonegiven in the problem
tox,eq equivalent oxide thickness, EOT, which Lectures 12 and 15 also called the effective oxide thickness: the thickness of SiO₂ that gives the same capacitance per unit area (nm or Å) · thigh-k physical thickness of the high-k film (the same unit) · εox = 3.9, the dielectric constant of SiO₂ · εhigh-k = k, that of the film (both dimensionless). The notes' form first, then M07's; any length unit works. A single uniform film; a stack needs (7.6). The film is electrically thinner than it is physically by k/3.9. The notes' case (p. 50): 10 nm at k = 20 gives 10 × 3.9/20 = 1.95 nm, "~2 nm"; Lecture 12 rounded 3.9 to 4 and got 2.0 nm exactly, a 2.5% change, while Wolf's equation for the example uses 3.9 (Eq. 4.1), the course value. Inverted, thigh-k = tox,eq × k/3.9: a 1.0 nm EOT needs 6.15 nm of HfO₂ (k = 24) or 2.31 nm of Al₂O₃ (k = 9). Lecture 15's problem, 1.4 nm of EOT at k = 30, needs 10.77 nm, about 110 Å, 7.7 times the EOT; at k = 24, 8.6 nm, and at 40, 14.4 nm. Use the k the problem states, and the notes' table value, 24 for HfO₂, where it states none. Tunnelling follows the physical thickness, (7.4); the transistor's vertical field follows the equivalent one. Beyond the notes: the field that fringes from the drain through the film's edges follows the physical thickness, so at fixed EOT the largest k is not automatically the best. Polysilicon depletion and the inversion layer each add a few ångströms in series (M07 §4; M06 §6.2). Taught in M07 §3; the argument is stated in M02 §4.
T ≈ exp (−2t√2m*qφBħ)
(7.4)
T probability that an electron at the silicon conduction-band edge crosses the dielectric (dimensionless) · t physical thickness of the dielectric (m) · m* electron effective mass inside the dielectric (kg) · qφB barrier height, the conduction-band offset between silicon and the dielectric (J; 1 eV = 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ J) · ħ = 1.055×10⁻³⁴ J·s. SI throughout. WKB estimate for a rectangular barrier at low bias; the current density is proportional to T. It shows the dependences only; the absolute current needs M06 §1. The exponent is linear in the physical thickness and grows as √(m*φB), and both are generally smaller in a high-k film than in SiO₂; Taur and Ning rank candidates by k√(m*φB) relative to SiO₂. The notes' result (p. 67): an HfO₂ capacitor at 12 Å EOT leaked four orders of magnitude less than 12 Å of SiO₂; its HfO₂ was 38 Å thick (Wolf), and its barrier about 1.4 eV against 3.1 eV. Wolf attributes the four decades to the physical thickness, 38 Å against 12 Å (p. 176); Lecture 15 tied them to the absence of the top interfacial layer. Taught in M07 §3.
1Ctot = 1C1 + 1C2
(7.5)
Ctot capacitance per unit area of the whole stack (F/cm²) · C1, C2 capacitances per unit area of the individual layers, each from (7.1) (F/cm²). The notes' form (p. 51). Extends to any number of layers by a term per layer. Holds when the layers carry no charge of their own between them; trapped charge shifts the threshold voltage but does not change the capacitance. Each 1/Ci = ti/(kiε0), so reciprocal capacitances add as thicknesses; multiplied through by 3.9ε0 this is (7.6). The same series rule as (6.3), where the second capacitor is the depleted polysilicon. Taught in M07 §4.
tox,eq = tSiO₂ + thigh-k (3.9k)
(7.6)
tox,eq equivalent oxide thickness of the stack · tSiO₂ physical thickness of the SiO₂ interfacial layer, which counts at full value because its k is 3.9 · thigh-k physical thickness of the high-k layer · k its dielectric constant (dimensionless). All thicknesses in one unit (nm or Å). The notes' form (p. 51) and Wolf's Eq. (4.3); in general tox,eq = Σ ti(3.9/ki), so an oxynitride interlayer enters with its own k. The notes (p. 50): the layer with the lowest k dominates the capacitance and sets the minimum EOT — raising k shrinks only the second term, so the stack is never electrically thinner than its interlayer. 0.5 nm of SiO₂ under 3.0 nm of HfO₂ (k = 24): 0.5 + 0.49 = 0.99 ≈ 1.0 nm. 0.6 nm under 3.8 nm: the high-k share is 1.65 nm at k = 9 and 0.62 nm at k = 24. Reading an interlayer out of a measurement: 12 Å EOT with 38 Å of HfO₂ leaves 12 − 6.2 = 5.8 Å, about 6 Å, which the micrographs show. Inverted: with 0.8 nm of interlayer a 1.0 nm EOT needs (1.0 − 0.8) × 24/3.9 = 1.23 nm of HfO₂, and 0.7 nm cannot be reached at any k. Lecture 12 put the interlayer at 1–2 nm (flagged, §2.3) without working the sum: under the notes' 10 nm film at k = 20, 1.5 nm of SiO₂ gives 1.5 + 1.95 = 3.45 nm, 43% of the EOT from 13% of the thickness, and 2.0 nm gives 3.95 nm, 51%. Taught in M07 §4; used for the memory capacitor in M07b §4.1.
St = 2.3 kTq (1 + CdCox)
(7.7)
St subthreshold swing, the gate voltage per decade of drain current (mV/decade) · 2.3 ≈ ln 10 · kT/q thermal voltage, 25.85 mV at 300 K · Cd capacitance per unit area of the depletion layer in the silicon at threshold (F/cm²) · Cox gate dielectric capacitance per unit area, from (7.1) or (7.5) (F/cm²). Long-channel form, Taur & Ning Eq. (5.40). Interface traps add Cit in parallel with Cd and raise the swing (M05b §3.7). The ideal, Cox ≫ Cd, is ln 10 × 25.85 mV = 59.5 mV/decade, the "60 mV/decade" no MOSFET can beat at room temperature. The notes (p. 51): 80 mV/decade can be achieved with Ta₂O₅, and a smaller St is better; Wolf calls values below 100 mV/decade very good, and Taur and Ning give 70–100 as typical. The notes' 80 is a short-channel figure (a 0.05 µm channel in Wolf's simulation, where SiO₂ reaches about 123); the excess over 60 is the drain's two-dimensional influence, which (7.7) does not contain. Below threshold the current falls one decade per St, so from threshold to zero gate voltage it falls VT/St decades, and a gate ΔV below threshold carries ID(VT) × 10−ΔV/St: Lecture 12's transistor, VT = 1.8 V with its gate at 1.6 V, is 2.5 decades, 316 times, below its threshold current at 80 mV/decade, and about 2300 times at 59.5. St is the inverse of the slope of log ID against VG (Taur & Ning). Taught in M07 §5.1.
t = Ncyc × g
(7.8)
t film thickness (Å) · Ncyc number of ALD cycles (dimensionless) · g growth per cycle (Å/cycle), a property of the precursor pair, the surface and the temperature, constant once the film has closed. The growth rate per unit time is g times the cycle frequency. Excludes the slow first cycles of nucleation on a bare surface. The notes (p. 65): a few ångströms per cycle at most, and 50–200 Å/min with gas switching under a second. The notes' reason, the lattice constant, is flagged in §2.3: adsorption saturates below a monolayer, and TMA with water grows 1.1–1.2 Å per cycle. The notes' 200 Å/min at Wolf's up to 4 cycles/s (240 cycles/min) gives g = 0.83 Å/cycle, and 38 Å takes about 46 cycles, about 11 s; at one cycle per second the same rate would need 3.3 Å/cycle. Taught in M07 §7.2.
ΔVT = φM,new − φM,old
(7.9)
ΔVT change in threshold voltage (V) · φM,new, φM,old work functions of the new and old gate materials, expressed in volts (4.6 eV enters as 4.6 V). From φMS = φM − φS, (3.3), with the silicon, the dielectric and its charges unchanged. n⁺ polysilicon ≈ 4.05 eV, the electron affinity of silicon; p⁺ polysilicon ≈ 4.05 + 1.12 = 5.17 eV; silicon midgap 4.05 + 1.12/2 = 4.61 eV (Robertson 4.6 eV), where a midgap metal such as TiN sits. n-channel, n⁺ poly to midgap metal: 4.6 − 4.05 ≈ +0.55 V, the notes' "about 0.6 V" (p. 60); p-channel, p⁺ poly to midgap: 4.6 − 5.17 ≈ −0.57 V, a larger negative threshold. A metal's effective work function on a high-k film can differ from its vacuum value. Lowering the raised threshold by counter-doping the channel gives a buried channel and worse short-channel behaviour (notes). Values in §16.12. Taught in M07 §9.

12 · High-k memory capacitors

M07b's equations carry no anchors, so each link below lands on the module section, with the subsection named beside it. Material and process values are in §16.13.

Vs = (Vnode − Vdd2) CcellCcell + Cbitline
(7b.1)
Vs read signal, the voltage difference between the bit line and its reference (V) · Vnode voltage stored on the cell, about Vdd for a "1" and 0 for a "0" (V) · Vdd supply voltage (V) · Ccell storage capacitance (F) · Cbitline bit-line capacitance (F); any consistent unit of capacitance works, since only the ratio enters. Ccell/(Ccell + Cbitline) is the transfer ratio. Assumes a bit line precharged to Vdd/2 and no charge lost during sharing. Taur & Ning Eq. (12.11); beyond the notes. A bit line of 256 to 1024 cells is of order 100–300 fF, so 30 fF gives 30/(30 + 300) ≈ 0.09 to 30/(30 + 100) ≈ 0.23; with Taur and Ning's ≈ 0.2, a cell at 1 V on a 1 V supply gives at most (1 − 0.5) × 0.2 ≈ 0.1 V. The signal scales with Ccell, which is the notes' sense-amplifier reason for holding the cell near 30 fF. Taught in M07b §1 and §2.2.
Q = CcellV,  N = CcellVq
(7b.2)
Q stored charge (C) · Ccell cell capacitance (F) · V voltage across the capacitor (V) · N number of stored electrons (dimensionless) · q = 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C. Scale: 1 fF × 1 V = 1 fC = 6.24×10³ electrons. A 30 fF cell holds 150 fC, 9.4×10⁵ electrons at 5 V; 90 fC, 5.6×10⁵ at 3 V; 30 fC, 1.9×10⁵ at 1 V. Inverted, V = Nq/Ccell: the notes' 1–2×10⁶ electrons per 30 fF cell (p. 52) would take 5.3–10.7 V, above every supply the passage mentions. The notes' pairing is stated as the notes give it; the arithmetic is the physics (flagged, §2.3). At fixed capacitance the charge falls with the voltage, five times from 5 V to 1 V. Lecture 12 divided 30 fF by q: the quotient, 1.9×10⁵, has the unit F/C = 1/V and is the count per volt, the number at 1 V. Retention budget (Taur & Ning): 30 fC losing under 10% in 100 ms allows about 30 fA of leakage. Taught in M07b §2 and §2.3.
Ccell = ε0kAt = 3.9ε0AEOT
(7b.3)
Ccell capacitance (F) · ε0 permittivity of free space, 8.854×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm = 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m — the form that matches the length unit · k dielectric constant of the film (dimensionless) · A electrode area actually covered by the dielectric (cm² or m²), in a three-dimensional capacitor the whole folded surface, not the footprint · t physical thickness (cm or m) · EOT = t(3.9/k), (7.3), or (7.6) for layered films. Parallel plate: neglects fringing at edges and corners. Planar, 30 fF on 10 nm of SiO₂: A = 8.7×10⁻¹² m² = 8.7 µm². A pedestal covered on its top and four sides: 0.15 µm × 0.45 µm × 0.3 µm high gives 0.0675 + 0.36 = 0.43 µm². 80 fF/µm² = 0.080 F/m² gives EOT = 3.9ε0/(C/A) = 0.43 nm. An interfacial SiO₂ at an electrode adds its full thickness to the EOT: 10 nm at k = 90 (0.43 nm) with 1 nm of SiO₂ becomes 1.43 nm, 30% of the intended capacitance. A trench with a square opening a and depth d has A = a2 + 4ad: 30 fF on 10 nm of SiO₂ (3.45 fF/µm², 8.69 µm²) at a = 0.5 µm needs d = (A − a2)/(4a) = 4.2 µm. Taught in M07b §3, §3.1 and §4.1.
R = R0 exp (−EakT),  ΔT×2 = kT2 ln 2Ea
(7b.4)
R deposition rate in the reaction-limited regime (e.g. nm/min) · R0 pre-exponential factor (same unit) · Ea activation energy (eV) · k Boltzmann constant, 8.617×10⁻⁵ eV/K to match Ea in eV (the J/K form, 1.381×10⁻²³, needs Ea in J) · T absolute temperature (K) · ΔT×2 the temperature rise that doubles the rate near T (K, equal to °C intervals). The second form follows from d(ln R)/dT = Ea/(kT2). Reaction-limited regime only; here k is Boltzmann's constant, not a dielectric constant. Ta₂O₅ from TAETO (Wolf): reaction-limited between 450 and 485 °C with Ea = 1.3 eV, mass-flow limited above 485 °C. At 467 °C (740 K): 8.617×10⁻⁵ × 740² × 0.693 ÷ 1.3 = 25 K, so the rate doubles every 25 °C. The reaction-limited regime is the conformal one, which is why the DRAM film is deposited there. Taught in M07b §5.1.
Ti% = [Ti][Ba] + [Sr] + [Ti] × 100
(7b.5)
[Ti], [Ba], [Sr] amounts of each cation in the film, in any common unit (atoms per unit volume, atomic per cent, moles) — only their ratio enters · Ti% titanium as a percentage of all cations (%). Oxygen is not counted. A stoichiometric perovskite, one A cation (Ba or Sr) per Ti, gives exactly 50%. The notes (p. 63): the highest capacitance density is at a slight excess, about 51%, and the titanium incorporated must be tightly controlled. Wolf: k drops by about 40% from 51% to 53%. As a Ti:A ratio, 51% is 0.51/0.49 = 1.04 and 53% is 0.53/0.47 = 1.13. Not the x of BaxSr1−xTiO₃, which is the barium fraction of the A sites, 0.5 < x < 0.7 in the notes. Taught in M07b §7.3.
tmask ≥ tfilm(1 + OE)S
(7b.6)
tmask initial mask thickness · tfilm thickness of the film to be removed, in the same unit · OE over-etch, as a fraction of the clearing time (0.1 for 10%) · S selectivity, the film's etch rate divided by the mask's (dimensionless: 1 for equal rates, below 1 when the mask etches faster). The mask left at the end is tmask − tfilm(1 + OE)/S; once the mask is gone, further etching thins the film beneath it. Assumes constant etch rates and a vertical attack, and ignores faceting. An etch is often run 10–20% past the time that just clears the film, because the rate and the thickness vary across the wafer and a conformal film is thicker, measured vertically, at the foot of a step (Plummer pp. 642–643); a purely physical etch such as ion milling reaches a selectivity of about two to one at best (p. 634). Lecture 14's cases at S = 1: 1.3 µm of metal on 1.0 µm of BST keeps 0.3 µm; mask and film both 1.0 µm with a 10% over-etch leave the patterned BST 0.9 µm thick, where the mask needed 1.1 µm. At the selectivities measured in an Ar/CF₄ plasma, 0.7 against resist and 0.6 against platinum, 0.30 µm of BST at a 20% over-etch needs 0.51 µm of resist or 0.60 µm of platinum. Beyond the notes, which say only that small features hinder ion milling (p. 64): M07b's construction from Plummer's definitions. Taught in M07b §9.2.

13 · Wafers and crystal growth

SiO₂ + 2C → Si + 2CO
Si + 3HCl ⇌ SiHCl₃ + H₂
(4.1)
(4.2)
Carbothermic reduction of silica in an electric arc furnace, giving metallurgical-grade silicon about 98% pure; then the reversible trichlorosilane reaction, run forward to make a distillable liquid (SiHCl₃ boils at 31.8 °C; crude SiHCl₃ about 99%, distilled to better than 99.9999%) and backward, in the Siemens reactor, to deposit polycrystalline silicon. Class values: 1400 °C for (4.1), 900 °C for both directions of (4.2), and six nines for electronic grade. Published values differ substantially (flagged, §2.3). Taught in M04 §1–2.
vrate ∝ 1Dcrystal
(4.3)

What it finds: Czochralski pull rate falls as the crystal diameter grows.

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
vrate
v_rate		Czochralski pull ratemm/minfound with this equation
Dcrystal
D_crystal		crystal diametermmgiven in the problem
vrate Czochralski pull rate (mm/min) · Dcrystal crystal diameter (mm). The relation as the notes give it: a larger diameter requires a slower pull because the latent heat released at the freezing front leaves through the crystal. The exponent is not −1: the latent heat and its conduction both scale with the cross-section, which cancels, and the pull rate is limited by radiative loss from the crystal's surface, giving vmax ∝ D−1/2. From 200 to 300 mm the notes' form predicts a 33% penalty and the correct form 18% (flagged, §2.3). Pull rates are below about 1 mm/min, and a 300 mm crystal takes up to 100 hours. Taught in M04 §3.
A300A200 = (300200)2 = 2.25
(4.4)

What it finds: Wafer area ratio: the square of the diameter ratio (300 vs 200 mm gives 2.25).

SymbolWhat it isUnitsValue or where to get it
A
A (wafer area)		wafer area; the ratio is the ratio of squared diameterscm²found with this equation
A wafer area (cm² or in²); the ratio is dimensionless, and in general it is the ratio of the squared diameters. A 300 mm wafer has 2.25 times the area of a 200 mm wafer. The usable die count rises by slightly more, because the fixed edge-exclusion zone is a smaller fraction of a larger wafer. Taught in M04 §4.
Si + 2NaOH + H₂O → Na₂SiO₃ + 2H₂
Si + 4NaOH → Na₄SiO₄ + 2H₂
(4.5)
The two silicate products the class gives for silicon CMP: sodium metasilicate, Na₂SiO₃, and sodium orthosilicate, Na₄SiO₄; which one dominates depends on the hydroxide concentration. The notes' mechanism: the frictional heat lets the NaOH oxidise the silicon, and colloidal silica particles about 10 nm across abrade the soft oxidised layer — hard enough to remove it (Knoop about 600) and softer than silicon (1150). This is why CMP removes material smoothly rather than scratching. Roughness falls from about 2.5 µm before polishing to less than 100 Å after. Taught in M04 §6.4.

14 · Lithography

2bmin = 3 √λ(s + d2)
(11.1)
2bmin minimum grating period (line + space), bmin minimum line width · λ exposure wavelength · s mask-to-resist gap (0 in hard contact) · d resist thickness. All in µm. Contact and proximity (shadow) printing only. The recorded class of 23 October; not in the notes or the course textbooks (Plummer eq. 5.12 gives the simpler √(λg)). Taught in M11 §2.1.
NA = sin θ  F = 0.5 λNA  DOF = 0.6 λNA2
(11.2)
NA numerical aperture (in air; n sin θ in general), < 1 · θ half-angle of the cone converging on the image · F minimum feature size and DOF total depth of focus, both in the unit of λ; focus within ±DOF/2. Notes p. 98 (written "DF"). Literature form k1λ/NA, k2λ/NA² (Plummer: Rayleigh k1 = 0.61, practical 0.6–0.8; DOF = ±λ/(2NA²)). Use the notes' 0.5 and 0.6. Not NA, the acceptor doping. Taught in M11 §3.
E = hcλ = 1239.84λ (nm) eV
(11.3)
Photon energy (eV) for a wavelength in nm; the same relation as (3.2) with λ in µm and 1.24. Taught in M11 §4.1.
nd = λ4 ⇒ d = λ4n
(11.4)
d BARC thickness · n BARC refractive index at the exposure wavelength · λ vacuum wavelength. Two passes give an extra path λ/2, so the substrate reflection cancels the reflection from the top of the BARC; 3λ/(4n) also works. Normal incidence, weakly absorbing layers, index rising resist → BARC → substrate; full cancellation also needs equal amplitudes (n = √(nresistnsub) for lossless layers). Notes p. 94. Taught in M11 §8.3.
d = λ2(n − 1)
(12.1)
d phase-shifter thickness (unit of λ) · λ vacuum exposure wavelength · n shifter index at λ. 180° relative to an equal path in air. Notes p. 99; Plummer eq. 5.16. Taught in M12 §4.2.
MEF = M ΔCDprintedΔCDmask
(12.2)
MEF (MEEF) mask error factor, 1 for simple demagnification · M lens reduction (4 for 4×) · ΔCD changes in printed and mask dimensions. Wolf eq. 7.2. Taught in M12 §4.5.
NA = n sin θ
(12.3)
n index of the medium between last lens and resist (1 air; ≈ 1.44 water at 193 nm) · θ half-angle of the converging cone. (11.2) is n = 1. Plummer eq. 5.3; immersion is from Lecture 19, not the notes. Taught in M12 §5.1.
d = λ2 sin θ
(12.4)
d multilayer period, one Mo/Si pair · λ EUV wavelength · θ glancing angle to the layers (90° at normal incidence). Refraction in the layers neglected. Wolf p. 304. Taught in M12 §8.1.
λ = 150V Å
(12.5)
λ electron de Broglie wavelength (Å) · V accelerating voltage (V). From h/√(2m0qV) = 12.26/√V Å, non-relativistic. Lecture 20; not in the notes. Taught in M12 §9.1.

14b · Chemical mechanical polishing

Numbered as in M13. Two βs appear here: the planarization factor (13.2) and the velocity exponent of (13.9); neither is the spreading-resistance β of M10. The three non-uniformities share (13.5) and differ only in which readings are averaged.

step coverage = tstn × 100 %
(13.1)
ts minimum thickness of the film where it crosses a step · tn nominal thickness on horizontal regions (same length unit). 100 % is ideal for a conductor. Wolf eq. 8.2; not in the notes, which name the condition only. Taught in M13 §1.2.
β = 1 − tfsteptistep
(13.2)
β planarization factor, dimensionless, 0 to 1 · tfstep final step height · tistep initial step height (same length unit). β = 0: no planarization or smoothing only; β = 1: complete planarization. Notes p. 107. This β is unrelated to the constant β (0.37–0.9) of the spreading resistance in M10 §3.4 and to the velocity exponent of (13.9). Taught in M13 §2.
RR = ΔTt, ΔT = Tpre − Tpost
(13.3)
RR removal rate (Å/min or nm/min) · ΔT thickness removed · Tpre, Tpost film thickness before and after polishing · t polishing time (min). Measured on an unpatterned wafer. Notes p. 113; Wolf eq. 8.3. Taught in M13 §5.1.
DOP = Hdi − HdfHdi × 100 %
(13.4)
DOP degree of planarization (%) · Hdi height of the feature before CMP · Hdf height of the feature after CMP (same length unit). Notes p. 113; Wolf eq. 8.4. Taught in M13 §5.2.
NU = σx̄, σ = (x1 − x̄)² + … + (xn − x̄)²n − 1
(13.5)
NU = WIDNU, WIWNU or WTWNU (dimensionless; often given in %) · x1 … xn thickness measurements within one die (WID), across one wafer (WIW) or one per wafer over n wafers (WTW) · x̄ their mean · σ their sample standard deviation (same unit as x). Notes pp. 113–116; Wolf eqs. 8.5–8.10. Taught in M13 §5.3.
%EOP = Tr − TdTr × 100
(13.6)
Tr dielectric thickness removed by CMP on top of a feature (e.g. a metal line) · Td oxide thickness lost at the centre of a wide space between metal lines (same length unit). 100 %: planar with no loss in the spaces. Notes p. 117; Wolf eq. 8.11a. Taught in M13 §5.4.
Ttotal = HmetalEOP + Tcov
(13.7)
Ttotal oxide thickness to deposit before CMP · Hmetal height of the metal lines · Tcov oxide thickness to remain over the lines (designer's target) · EOP efficiency as a fraction (%EOP/100). Assumes a conformal deposit and a planar final surface. Wolf eqs. 8.11b and 8.12; not in the notes. Taught in M13 §5.4.
RR = Kp P v
(13.8)
RR removal rate (m/s in SI) · P applied pressure (Pa = N/m²) · v linear velocity of the pad relative to the wafer (m/s) · Kp Preston's constant, independent of P and v; with these units it is in Pa⁻¹ (m²/N). Process data come in psi and ft/min: 1 psi = 6894.76 Pa, 1 ft/min = 5.08×10⁻³ m/s. Notes p. 118; Wolf eq. 8.13. Taught in M13 §6.1.
RR = Kp Pαvβ
(13.9)
α, β adjustable exponents, determined experimentally for the system polished; other symbols as in (13.8), with the units of Kp now depending on α and β. This β is the notes' symbol for the velocity exponent and is unrelated to the planarization factor β of eq. (13.2). Notes p. 118; Wolf eq. 8.14. Taught in M13 §6.2.
RR = Kp v P2/3
(13.10)
Zhao and Shi's oxide removal rate: linear in velocity, sub-linear in pressure. Kp here has different units from (13.8). Notes p. 118; Wolf eq. 8.15; B. Zhao and F. G. Shi, Electrochem. Solid-State Lett. 2, 145 (1999). Taught in M13 §6.2.
RR = Kp v (P2/3 − Pth2/3) for P > Pth; RR = 0 for P < Pth
(13.11)
Pth threshold pressure below which abrasive particles do not slide against the surface. Wolf eqs. 8.16–8.17, after Zhao and Shi; not in the notes. Taught in M13 §6.2.

Numbered as in M14. Db (14.3) is the part of the dishing D of (14.2) that the barrier step produces. In (14.6)–(14.8) A is the aspect ratio t/w of a line, not a wafer area, and lengths are in metres.

SA:B = RRARRB
(14.1)
SA:B selectivity of material A to material B, written "S:1" (dimensionless) · RRA, RRB removal rates of A and B (M13 eq. 13.3) in the same unit, for example nm/min, measured under the same polishing conditions. A slurry with high selectivity of the polished film to the film beneath lets the polish stop on the lower film. Taught in M14 §1.1.
tm = h − (D + E)
(14.2)
tm final thickness of the metal in the trench · h depth of the trench as etched, measured from the original dielectric surface · D dishing, the recess of the metal below the dielectric surface beside it · E erosion, the loss of dielectric thickness during the metal polish. All in the same length unit (nm or µm). The barrier thickness, where it matters, is subtracted separately (eq. 14.4). Taught in M14 §5.3.
Db = tb · RRCuRRb − tb = tb(SCu:b − 1)
(14.3)
Db recess of the trench copper below the dielectric surface produced by the barrier step (nm), the D of eq. (14.2) · tb barrier thickness on the field (nm) · RRCu, RRb removal rates of copper and barrier (nm/min) · SCu:b their selectivity (eq. 14.1); the copper removed is tbSCu:b. Assumes the pad presses on the trench copper as hard as on the field, which holds only in the middle of a line much wider than the pad's sag. One contribution only: the overpolish, dishing left by the copper step, and chemical or galvanic removal add to it. Taught in M14 §5.3.
R = ρCuL(W − 2tb)(h − tb − D − E)
(14.4)
R line resistance (Ω) · ρCu copper resistivity, 1.7 µΩ·cm for bulk copper (Wolf; Plummer) · L line length · W trench width · h trench depth · tb barrier thickness on the walls and floor · D, E dishing and erosion (eq. 14.2). Lengths in cm when ρ is in Ω·cm. Assumes a rectangular copper core, the barrier carrying no current, and D as the mean recess across the line. Taught in M14 §5.4.
tf = T0 − TfRR
(14.5)
tf polishing time (min) · T0 measured thickness before polishing · Tf required final thickness (both nm, or µm × 1000) · RR removal rate from the calibration (nm/min), the rate of M13 eq. 13.3 rearranged. Taught in M14 §6.1.
Rint = ρlwt
(14.6)
Rint line resistance (Ω) · ρ metal resistivity (Ω·m) · l, w, t length, width and thickness of the line (m). Wolf eq. 12.2; the same law as eq. (14.4) without the barrier. Taught in M14 §8.1.
Cint = 2kε0l (wd + ts)
(14.7)
Cint capacitance of one line to its surroundings (F) · k relative dielectric constant of the insulator (dimensionless) · ε0 = 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m · d dielectric thickness to the ground planes · s spacing to the neighbouring lines (m). Wolf eqs. 12.3–12.5, parallel-plate terms only, fringing neglected. Taught in M14 §8.1.
RintCint = 8ρkε0 l2p2 (1A2 + 1)
(14.8)
RintCint RC time constant of the line (s) · ρ (Ω·m) · k (dimensionless) · ε0 (F/m) · l line length and p pitch (same unit, so l/p is a pure number) · A = t/w. Wolf eq. 12.10; valid for w = s and d = t. The 1/A² term is the vertical capacitance, the 1 the horizontal. Taught in M14 §8.1.

14c · Shallow trench isolation

Numbered as in M15. Field threshold of a trench: (8.6) with the trench depth as tFOX.

xSi = 0.46 tox,  habove = 0.54 tox
(15.1)
xSi silicon consumed, below the original surface · tox thermal oxide grown · habove oxide above the original surface (all nm). The course's ratio (Plummer 0.45; 0.44–0.46 by oxide density, M05a §4). Any thermal oxide: LOCOS field oxide, STI liner, sacrificial oxide. Taught in M15 §1.1.
ΔtN = f · tclearS
(15.2)
ΔtN nitride removed in the overpolish (nm) · f overpolish as a fraction of the clearing time · tclear oxide the polish must clear over the nitride (nm) · S oxide-to-nitride selectivity. Constant oxide rate, all the nitride exposed for the whole overpolish. An etchback lowers tclear and so shortens the polish; selectivity reduces only the nitride lost. Taught in M15 §3.3.
VT,sw = φMS − QiCtr + |Qd|Ctr + 2φFp,  Ctr = 3.9ε0wtr,  φMS = −(φFn + φFp)
(15.3)
VT,sw well voltage at which the p-type sidewall inverts, referred to the grounded n⁺ regions (V) · wtr trench width (cm) · φFn, φFp Fermi potentials of the n-well and the p-type sidewall · Qi as in (8.6) · |Qd| = √(2εSiqNA(2φFp + VSB)), VSB the reverse bias of the body below the n⁺ regions. The notes' one-dimensional sidewall MOSFET. Taught in M15 §5.2.

16.3 Bonds and electronegativity

BondIonic characterLengthEnergy (kJ/mol)Energy (eV per bond)
Si–Si0%2.35 Å (crystal)2222.30
Si–H2%1.48 Å3183.30
Si–N28%not given3553.68
Si–O45%1.62 Å (SiO₂)4524.68
Si–F66%1.55 Å (SiF₄)5655.86

Energies are mean single-bond values from one compilation (Huheey), a comparison of order; a particular bond at a particular site departs from its mean — a hydrogen on an interfacial Pb site on (111) dissociates at about 2.83 eV. The ordering Si–F > Si–O > Si–N > Si–H > Si–Si is what decides which terminator survives heat or hot carriers. Electronegativities, Pauling scale as revised by Allred: Si 1.90, B 2.04, H 2.20, N 3.04, O 3.44, F 3.98; Sze & Ng plot Pauling's 1960 values (Si 1.8, O 3.5). Valence: Si 4, O 2, N 3, H 1, F 1; B forms three bonds, or four on accepting an electron. M05a §1; M05b §4.3–4.4.

16.4 Oxide and interface charge

QuantityValueNote and source
Interface charge density Ni5×10¹⁰ to 10¹³ cm⁻² Depending on process quality (M03 §3)
Trap density on a clean bare surfaceAbout 10¹⁵ cm⁻² Of the order of the surface atom density (M05b §3.2)
Pb density on oxidised (111)About 10¹³ cm⁻² Roughly constant for oxidation at 300–800 °C, falling above; tracks oxide stress (M05b §3.2)
After a hydrogen annealAs low as 10¹⁰ cm⁻², about one trap per 10⁵ surface atoms Sze & Ng; the lecture's target is one in 10⁴ (M05b §3.3)
Pb charge transitions (spin resonance)Ev + 0.31 eV (+/0) and Ev + 0.80 eV (0/−) Dry-oxidised (111), Poindexter et al. 1984: 0.49 eV apart, about 0.25 eV either side of midgap, Ev + 0.56 eV; 1.5×10¹³ cm⁻² eV⁻¹. Notes: "a deep trap level near the Si midgap" (M05b §3.2)
Interface-trap density peaks (C–V)Ev + 0.26 and Ev + 0.84 eV Same samples, 2×10¹³ cm⁻² eV⁻¹, about 0.3 eV either side of midgap: the density of traps of every kind, not the transitions of one defect. Their agreement with the spin-resonance pair is the evidence that Pb is a major source of interface traps (M05b §3.2)
Orientation(100): about ⅓ the fixed charge of (111), and an order of magnitude lower Dit Fixed charge (111) > (110) > (100) (M05b §3.6)
Fixed charge positionWithin about 2 nm of the interfacePlummer (M05b §2)
Oxygen vacancy Si–Si distanceAbout 2.5 Å (2.3–2.7 Å) Bulk amorphous SiO₂ calculation; against 2.35 Å in the crystal and 3.12 Å across a bridging O. The vacancy becomes the E′ centre on hole capture (M05b §3.4, §5.2)
Junction leakage cut by a thin oxide10–100×Atalla, Bell Labs, 1958 (M05b §3.7)
Chlorine in the oxidation ambientHCl or TCA, a small additive to the O₂; 6% HCl in the notes' 4 nm recipe 4HCl + O₂ ⇌ 2H₂O + 2Cl₂; the chlorine carries trace metals out as volatile chlorides, and the chlorine taken into the oxide, mostly near the Si/SiO₂ interface, gives some protection against Na⁺ (Plummer). TCA is trichloro­ethane. Lecture 10: it immobilises Na⁺ and K⁺, for reasons not explained (M05b §6, M06 §9.3)

16.5 Anneals

Anneal and chargeTemperatureAmbientPlace in the flow and notes
Hydrogen anneal (post-metallization, final, sinter or alloy anneal): Qit 400–450 °C (lecture; Plummer, about 30 min); ≈450 °C (Wolf) 100% H₂, or 4% H₂ in N₂, called forming gas (notes p. 26; Wolf p. 85) After metallization, the last thermal step; the 450 °C ceiling is set by the aluminium. Lecture 7: 3% H₂ in 97% Ar (flagged, §2.3); Plummer: 10% H₂ in N₂. Non-flammable ceilings: about 5.5% H₂ in N₂, 3.1% in Ar; hydrogen burns in air at 4–75%. M05b §4.1
Oxide-trap anneal: Qot Above 550 °C (notes p. 29; Wolf p. 90) Inert, N₂ or Ar (lecture) Before metallization; "low temperature" relative to oxidation at 1000–1100 °C. Some neutral traps may remain. Lecture 8: about 500 °C (flagged, §2.3). M05b §5.4
Fixed-charge anneal: Qf High temperature, at the end of high-temperature processing Inert; argon preferred to nitrogen Before metallization; the hydrogen anneal does not change Qf. M05b §2
Deuterium variant: Qit As the hydrogen annealD₂ in place of H₂ More than ten times less hot-electron degradation (M05b §4.3)
Oxygen out-diffusion (denuded zone) Above 1200 °C— Clears oxide precipitates from the surface layer of a Czochralski wafer (M04 §3)

Batch furnace limitDifficult below 2 nm (notes) Low temperatures that slow growth degrade quality (M06 §8.2)

RTO advantages (notes)Lower leakage up to 1050 °C growth; boron resistance (2 nm at 1000 °C RTP against 800 °C); higher mobility from 800 to 1000 °C M06 §8.2

16.7 Nitrided and fluorinated oxides

QuantityValueNote and source
Nitriding ambientsNH₃, N₂O, NO, or O₂ + NO (notes) N₂ is too inert. The film is an oxynitride, SiOxNy, not Si₃N₄ (M06 §9.1)
N at the Si/SiO₂ interfaceAbout 1–2 atomic % Associated with higher breakdown field and lower Qf (M06 §9.1)
N at the poly/SiO₂ interfaceAbove about 5 atomic % Needed to block boron from the p⁺ gate; nitrogen in the bulk degrades the film (M06 §9.1)
Direct N₂O growth by RTPN peak about 0.5 atomic % at Si/SiO₂ (Wolf) Thickness ∝ (time)1/2; practical only below 100 Å; peak mobility about 5% lower, high-field mobility about 10% higher (M06 §9.1, §9.3)
N₂O dissociationNeeds above 850 °C (notes) Products NO 4.7%, N₂ 64.3%, O₂ 31% (Wolf); only the NO nitrides (M06 §9.3)
Two-step recipe, furnace (notes p. 40)50 Å of SiO₂ at 850 °C for 30 min; an N₂O anneal at 950 °C then adds 35 Å, 85 Å in all The N₂O step nitrides the Si/SiO₂ interface and oxidises, supplying 35/85 = 41% of the film. Lecture 10 ran the sequence by RTP in about 30 s per step, too short to grow these thicknesses: 30 s at 900 °C grows about 0.65 nm (flagged, §2.3; M06 §9.3)
Two-step profile (notes' drawing)10% O₂ at 900 °C; then 100% N₂O at 950 °C, then 100% N₂ at 950 °C A second furnace study (Wolf Fig. 3-32); the N₂ step is an inert anneal. Lecture 10 taught the drawing and the recipe as one sequence (M06 §9.3)
Low-pressure N₂O1–50 Torr (notes)A minimum pressure is needed before N is incorporated (notes). Lecture 10: RTP at 1–20 Torr, NO + O₂ at 800–850 °C (flagged, §2.3; M06 §9.3)
NO recipe4 nm in O₂ at 800 °C with 6% HCl, N₂ anneal; then diluted NO at 800 °C for 45 min NO gives more nitrogen at lower thermal budget. A furnace operation (Wolf); Lecture 10 ran both steps as RTA (M06 §9.3)
Fluorine sources (notes)A BF₂⁺ implant into source and drain; LPCVD-W or WSix from WF₆ and SiH₄ Both leave fluorine in or on the polysilicon gate, and later anneals drive it to the Si/SiO₂ interface; fluorine is also implanted into the gate deliberately (M06 §9.4)
BF₂⁺ implantOne B and two F per ion; the boron takes 11/49 of the energy From BF₃ feed gas, mass-analysed; the shallow source/drain extension implant (Wolf). 2.2 keV BF₂⁺ places boron as 0.5 keV B⁺ does; at a nominal 1×10¹⁵ cm⁻² it loses about 20% of its dose to sputtering, against 10% for the B⁺ (M06 §9.4)
CVD tungsten, (6.11)250–500 °C, 0.1–2 Torr; fluorine retained often 1 atomic % or more At 1 atomic % about one fluorine kept per six hundred brought in by the WF₆; WSi₂ with a high SiH₄:WF₆ ratio (Plummer; M06 §9.4)
Fluorine doseKept below 1×10¹⁴ cm⁻² (notes), for fluorine put into the gate The study behind it co-implanted fluorine into the p⁺ polysilicon gate on purpose and used 1×10¹⁴ cm⁻² itself, a "medium dose": "at or below" (Chen et al., Electrochem. Solid-State Lett. 3, 290 (2000); flagged, §2.3; M06 §9.4)
Nitrogen implant for a dual oxide10–30 keV, 2×10¹⁴ to 1×10¹⁵ cm⁻² (Wolf) Slows local oxidation; one thermal oxidation grows both thicknesses. Lecture 10 kept the first oxide and oxidised again (flagged, §2.3; M06 §9.5)

Notes' values from pp. 49, 54, 58 and 66–67; ⚑ marks a notes value that differs within the notes or from the literature (§2.3). Literature: Robertson (2004) Table 2 for k, gaps and conduction-band offsets, except the HfO₂ k range (Taur & Ning) and the ALD Al₂O₃ offset (Robertson Table 5); TiO₂ and SrTiO₃ are the two points Robertson adds to show where the k–Eg trend leads. SiO₂'s offsets are the course's 3.1 and 4.6 eV (Taur & Ning), Robertson's 3.2 eV in brackets. The Ta₂O₅ offset is Robertson's calculated value, 0.3 eV measured; the perovskites' offsets are near zero. M07 takes each other valence-band offset as Robertson's gap less 1.1 eV less the conduction-band offset. Subthreshold swingIdeal 59.5 mV/decade at 300 K; 80 mV/decade with Ta₂O₅ (notes p. 51) Wolf's simulation: at 0.05 µm, 80 for Ta₂O₅ and about 123 for SiO₂; at 0.1 µm about 65 and 75; from 0.2 µm both near 60. Below 100 "very good" (Wolf); 70–100 typical (Taur & Ning). Each swing below threshold divides the current by ten: 0.2 V at 80 mV/decade is 316 times, about 2300 at 59.5 (Lecture 12's transistor; M07 §5.1)

Lecture 15's routes flagged: TMA + O₂ by MOCVD, and "aluminium tetra­chloride" with O₂ "mixed in water" by ALD — the chloride is AlCl₃, a solid whose vapour is pulsed with water vapour, 2AlCl₃ + 3H₂O → Al₂O₃ + 6HCl. Lectures 11 and 15: k 9–11 (M07 §8.2)
ZrO₂Reactive sputtering in O₂ + Ar, or RTA oxidation of Zr; HF-etchable; Al doping by co-sputtering; anneal 400–600 °C in O₂ or inert gas (notes p. 66) ALD from ZrCl₄ + H₂O at about 300 °C (Robertson); Zr-Al-Si-O; yttria-stabilised ZrO₂. Co-sputtering: targets on separate guns, composition set by gun power. In the lectures' account a ZrO₂ target alone gives ZrO1.8 to ZrO1.9, and 3–5% O₂ is added to the argon (5–10% for BST) (M07 §7.1, §7.2, §8.3)
HfO₂PVD or in-situ RTCVD; poly below 600 °C and RTA at 1000 °C; crystallises; 12 Å EOT, 10⁴ less leakage than SiO₂ (notes pp. 66–67) 38 Å HfO₂ with about 6 Å interlayer (Wolf), the four decades from the physical thickness (Wolf p. 176; Lecture 15 credited the missing top interlayer); TaN gate: EOT 8 Å. The 600 °C is for depositing the polysilicon; Lecture 15 put it on the HfO₂ (flagged). For k, use a problem's value: Lecture 15 quoted 25–30 and worked at 30 (M07 §3, §8.4, §9)
Pr₂O₃5 nA/cm² at 1 V, EOT 14 Å, k ≈ 31, no C–V hysteresis; MBE at 625–725 °C, 0.1 Å/s; 600 °C in dry N₂ or RTA at 1000 °C for 15 s in N₂ (notes p. 67) Furnace anneal 5 min (Wolf) (M07 §8.6)
