16.13 DRAM and FeRAM capacitors

QuantityValueNote and source
Cell capacitanceAbout 30 fF per cell (notes p. 52) Taur & Ning: 30 fF typical. The notes pair it with 1–2×10⁶ electrons, which Q = CV does not give (flagged, §2.3). Lecture 12's 30 fF ÷ q = 1.9×10⁵ is electrons per volt, the count at 1 V (M07b §2, §2.3)
Supply5 V → 3 V, may drop to 1 V (notes p. 52)M07b §2
Why 30 fF staysSoft errors; sense-amplifier detection; retention time (notes pp. 52–53) α-particles from U and Th in packaging; cosmic rays, at ground level secondary neutrons (M07b §2)
α-particle charge5 MeV stopping in Si → about 1.4×10⁶ pairs at 3.6 eV per pair May & Woods 1979 (M07b §2.1)
Bit line256–1024 cells; 100–300 fF; transfer ratio about 0.09–0.23, Taur & Ning ≈ 0.2 Beyond the notes (M07b §1)
RetentionWorst-case specification about 100 ms; about 30 fA of total leakage for 30 fC and a 10% loss Pass transistor: VT at least about 0.7 V at 25 °C, gate oxide thicker than about 2 nm (Taur & Ning). DDR4: every row within 64 ms up to 85 °C and 32 ms from 85 to 95 °C, about 16 times a second (JEDEC JESD79-4) (M07b §1)
Planar capacitor30 fF on 10 nm SiO₂: 8.7 µm² Trench or stack since the 4 Mb generation, 1.2 µm features (Taur & Ning; M07b §3)
Trench capacitorAbout 5 µm deep (Lecture 13); A = a2 + 4ad 30 fF on 10 nm SiO₂ at a 0.5 µm opening: 4.2 µm, 35 times the footprint. Lecture 13's steps: trench by RIE, the S/D n⁺ diffusion dopes the wall (the storage electrode), one oxidation for gate and capacitor oxide, polysilicon fill as the plate (M07b §3.1)
ONO50 Å (notes p. 53)Wolf's minimum: tunnelling excessive below about 5 nm (M07b §3.2)
HSG (RSP) polysiliconArea × about 2 (notes) ONO with HSG at 0.18 and 0.15 µm; not beyond 256 Mb, the notes' "256 MB" (M07b §3.3)
0.15 µm stacked cellPedestal 0.15 × 0.45 µm, 0.3 µm high: 0.43 µm²; about 80 fF/µm², EOT about 0.43 nmWolf p. 162 (M07b §3)
Electrodes consideredAl, Ir, Pt, Ru, TaN, TiN, W (notes p. 55) SIS, MIS, MIM; poly grows interfacial SiO₂ that adds to the EOT. MIS reads top-down, metal–insulator–silicon (Wolf p. 152); Lecture 12 said SIM. SIS in five steps (Lecture 13): polysilicon from SiH₄ by LPCVD (575–650 °C, Plummer; the upper part of the lecture's 600–800 °C lies above it), oxidise, high-k, TiN barrier, polysilicon plate. Thin-film TiN: 50–150 µΩ·cm (§16.10) (M07b §4.1)
RuO₂ρ = 35 µΩ·cm (notes p. 59) About thirteen times Al's 2.7 µΩ·cm; single crystals 30–40 µΩ·cm (M07b §4.3)
RuO₄Melts at 25.4 °C; toxicAn oxidiser, not flammable (flagged; M07b §4.3)
Pt costAbout $1000 per 200 mm wafer for 50 nm sputtered at 40% efficiency CVD: about 20% of the precursor deposits, about 1% remains on the finished wafer (Wolf; M07b §4.2)
Ta₂O₅ k25 (pp. 54, 58); 26 (p. 49); 22 (p. 56) 65 when crystallised on Ru (Wolf) (M07b §5, §5.4)
Ta₂O₅ CVDTAETO or TATDMAE + O₂; 1–4 Torr, 450–485 °C (notes p. 58, the method unnamed; LPCVD in Wolf) Precursors vaporised at about 170 °C, carried by Ar; Ea 1.3 eV, reaction-limited 450–485 °C, mass-flow limited above 485 °C; rate × 2 per 25 °C (Wolf). Lecture 13's 480–500 °C, its MOCVD name and its halide LPCVD route are flagged (M07b §5.1)
Anodic Ta₂O₅About 1.7 nm per volt of forming voltage (Gill) Ta film the anode (+), Pt the cathode (−), in dilute acid; 2Ta + 5H₂O → Ta₂O₅ + 5H₂. The oxide takes about 2.5 times the tantalum's volume, so about 40% of it lies below the original surface. The dielectric of tantalum capacitors (M07b §5.1)
Nitrided interlayer (RTN)"An interfacial layer of SiO₂ … achieved by rapid thermal (polysi) nitridation" (notes p. 58): oxidise, then RTA in N₂O (Lecture 13); or NH₃ at 900 °C for 60 s on bare polysilicon (Wolf) Its nitrogen retards further oxidation rather than stopping it (Wolf p. 118); the EOT rises after oxygen anneals above about 700 °C, and above about 800 °C silicon diffuses through (M07b §5.2, §5.3)
Ta₂O₅ densificationO₂ anneal or RTA in oxygen at 600–700 °C; also crystallises the film (notes p. 58) Wolf: UV-ozone furnace at 500 °C, or O₂ plasma at 600 °C then above 700 °C (leakage at 1 V down by up to six orders); peak below about 800 °C; one process kept every later step at or below 500 °C. Lecture 13's 900–1000 °C is above that ceiling (flagged). Ta2O4.9, Lecture 13's example, has 2% of its oxygen sites empty (M07b §5.3)
Ta₂O₅ EOT10 nm on poly → 2.5 nm; minimum practical about 1.5 nm (Wolf) Production MIS Ta₂O₅ about 3.5 nm (Saino 2004) (M07b §5, §5.5)
Ta₂O₅ reachUp to 4 Gb (p. 54); with HSG, 1 Gb and 4 Gb, 0.15 µm (p. 59) Wolf: may be 1 Gb (flagged; M07b §5.5)
Ru/Ta₂O₅/RuAnnealed Ta₂O₅ k = 65 Much lower leakage after forming gas at 400 °C (Wolf; M07b §5.4)

BST roughnessLowers k, raises leakage (notes p. 63) Less haze above 600 °C (Wolf) (M07b §7.3)
BST conformalityMOCVD 80%; sputtering and sol-gel 60% (notes p. 61) Wolf: MOCVD can exceed 80%, the others typically below 60% (M07b §8.1)
BST CVD temperature480–550 °C (notes p. 62) Crossover about 480 °C (Wolf's figure: about 475 °C); non-adducted precursors: 100% conformal at 480 °C, above 100 fF/µm², 10⁻⁸ A/cm² range; adducted: about 550 °C, poor conformality (flagged). Lecture 14's 500–700 °C MOCVD furnace is the notes' anneal range (flagged; M07b §8.3)
BST CVD precursorsβ-diketonate (THD) adducts of Ba and Sr with an organometallic Ti compound, in an organic solvent (notes p. 62) Lecture 14's BaCl₂, SrCl₂ and TiCl₄ in water are flagged: the Ba and Sr chlorides melt only above 850 °C and have no useful vapour near 500 °C; TiCl₄ boils at 136 °C but hydrolyses (M07b §8.2)
BST annealAfter the top electrode, 500–700 °C, in N₂O, O₂ or N₂ (notes p. 62) About 550 °C for films grown near 550 °C, closer to 700 °C for low-temperature ones; capacitance up about 25% (Wolf) (M07b §8.4)
BST electrodesPt, Ir, Ru, IrO₂, RuO₂; Pt on a Ti/TiN barrier (notes p. 63) · top: Pt, Ru, RuO₂, IrO₂, SrRuO₃, (La,Sr)CoO₃ (notes p. 64) No Al above 600 °C (notes; Wolf 500 °C), in either electrode, since the anneal follows the top one; no TiN above 500 °C, no poly (Wolf); Pt as top flagged (M07b §9.1)
BST etchingNo volatile products: ion milling (notes p. 64) TiF₄ is volatile, BaF₂ and SrF₂ are not; Lecture 14's all-volatile CF₄ etch is flagged. Ion milling: a broad Ar beam, selectivity about 2:1 at best, so the mask follows (7b.6). Ru and RuO₂ etch in O₂/Cl₂ via volatile RuO₄; substrate above 350 °C helps (Wolf) (M07b §9.2)
FeRAM dielectricsPZT and SBT, k = 1000–1500 (notes p. 57) PZT's value (Wolf); SBT is SrBi₂Ta₂O₉, films about 180–300 (flagged; M07b §10.1). The name ferro­electric, an analogy with ferro­magnetism, was coined by Schrödinger in 1912; Valasek reported the effect in Rochelle salt in 1920 (M07b §6.2)

The notes' DRAM stack-capacitor roadmap (p. 56), from Wolf's Table 4-2, about 1999. The k values are requirements, not material properties; "Epi BST" is epitaxial BST. Production took a different path: MIS Ta₂O₅ and Al₂O₃, then HfO₂- and ZrO₂-based films (M07b §4.4). The node row falls about 0.7 times per generation, Lecture 13's rule: the cell area halves and, at 30 fF, the capacitance per unit footprint doubles each node, 35 times over the five steps. Lecture 13 dictated Ta₂O₅ at 25 in both Ta₂O₅ columns and SBT at 1500 in the "?" columns; both are flagged, and a DRAM dielectric must not be ferroelectric.

16.14 Wells and well implants

QuantityValueNote and source
Diffused wellImplant at 50–100 keV, then 12–24 h above 1100 °C; 3–4 µm deep (notes pp. 68–70) Doping highest at the surface, falling mono­tonically; lateral spread about 0.7 times the depth, 2–3 µm; aspect ratio at most about 1.6:1 (notes p. 69). Implanted and driven in before the LOCOS field oxide, and driven further during it (M08 §2, §8)
Retrograde wellImplant at 300 keV–1 MeV (notes p. 70); peak at roughly 1 µm (Wolf) Lateral spread after all processing about 1 µm, against at least 2 µm for a diffused well (Wolf); lateral straggle at 500 keV ~0.7 µm (notes, Wolf); only a short activation anneal; implanted after the field oxide (M08 §4)
n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing, 1.2 µm technology7–9 µm with diffused wells → 5 µm with retrograde wells (notes p. 71) 29–44% less; the chained-implant process reports 40% lower (notes p. 74) (M08 §4.5, §6.3)
Chained recipe, n-well (notes pp. 73–74)Phosphorus: 600 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻² (well); 150 keV, 4×10¹² (punch-through and channel stop); 70 keV, 6×10¹² (threshold) SRIM-2013 Rp 0.73, 0.20, 0.095 µm; ΔRp 0.144, 0.063, 0.036 µm; peaks 8.3×10¹⁷, 2.5×10¹⁷, 6.6×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, each implant alone in bare silicon (M08 §6.3)
Chained recipe, p-well (notes pp. 73–74)Boron: 300 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻² (well); 150 keV, 4×10¹² (punch-through and channel stop); 30 keV, 5.5×10¹² (threshold) Rp 0.79, 0.45, ≈ 0.11 µm; ΔRp 0.133, 0.105, ≈ 0.048 µm (30 keV inter­polated); peaks 9.0×10¹⁷, 1.5×10¹⁷, ≈ 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (M08 §6.3)
Chain order and toolingAll implants in one implanter, highest energy first (notes p. 73); resist 2.0–2.5 µm (notes pp. 72, 74) So that no ion crosses an earlier implant's end-of-range damage (Wolf p. 186); the implanter spans about 1 MeV to 10 keV (Wolf p. 187); DRAM wells take higher doses (notes p. 73). Resist by (8.13): 2.05 µm for 600 keV P, 2.08 µm for 300 keV B, 2.84 µm for 500 keV B (M08 §6)
Projected ranges in silicon (SRIM-2013)Boron: 0.17–0.32 µm at 50–100 keV, 0.79 µm at 300 keV, 1.13 µm at 500 keV · phosphorus: 0.07–0.13 µm at 50–100 keV, 0.73 µm at 600 keV Phosphorus needs about twice boron's energy for the same depth (Plummer p. 62); straggles 0.133 µm (B, 300 keV), 0.15 µm (B, 500 keV), 0.144 µm (P, 600 keV) (M08 §4.1, §6.3)
Lateral straggle ΔR⊥ (SRIM-2013)Boron 0.15 µm at 300 keV, 0.18 µm at 500 keV; phosphorus 0.14 µm at 600 keV The notes' ~0.7 µm at 500 keV is about four of these (flagged, §2.3); a 3×10¹³ cm⁻² boron implant at 500 keV reaches 0.53 µm under the mask at 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ (M08 §4.2)
Ranges in the field oxide (SRIM-2013)Boron about 10% farther in SiO₂ than in silicon; phosphorus up to about 6% farther 0.3 µm of field oxide does the work of 0.27–0.28 µm of silicon for boron and 0.28–0.30 µm for phosphorus. Under it the chained p-well's surface is about 4×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ (field threshold about 7 V) and the n-well's about 8×10¹⁶ cm⁻³; the well peaks sit 0.44–0.51 µm below the silicon surface (M08 §6.4)
Intrinsic diffusivities (Plummer Table 7-3)D0 / EA: boron 1.0 cm²/s / 3.5 eV; phosphorus 4.70 cm²/s / 3.68 eV At 1100 °C: B 1.4×10⁻¹³, P 1.5×10⁻¹³ (1.46×10⁻¹³) cm²/s, arsenic 2.1×10⁻¹⁴, about seven times slower than P; at 1000 °C: P 1.27×10⁻¹⁴ cm²/s. Plummer's worked example reads 1.5×10⁻¹³ for boron at 1100 °C (M08 §2.1, §4.3)
Segregation coefficient m = CSi/CSiO₂ Boron about 0.3; phosphorus, arsenic and antimony about 10 (Plummer §7.5.4) Boron is taken into a growing oxide, phosphorus piles up ahead of it (M08 §3.2)
AnnealsImplant damage: about 10 s at 1000 °C, or about 30 min at 800 °C (Plummer p. 61) Lecture 16: 800–900 °C, or about 1000 °C briefly. Early retrograde twin wells: RTP well "drive-ins" of about 5 min (Wolf p. 186) (M08 §4.3)
Channel stopBoron, about 10¹³ cm⁻² at 50 keV, through the LOCOS nitride openings before field oxidation (Plummer p. 64) In the diffused-well flow, under the field oxide on the p-well only; in the chain the 150 keV implants serve, their dose at the surface beneath the field oxide (M08 §3.3, §6.4)
Latch-up triggerAbout 0.7 V across Rwell or Rsub (Plummer p. 467) Suppressed by a lightly doped epitaxial layer on a heavily doped substrate, a buried layer or a retrograde well's heavy bottom (M08 §5.1)
Wafer, as Lectures 15–16 gave itp⁺ substrate about 10¹⁹ cm⁻³; epitaxial layer about 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, 3–5 µm thick in earlier processes and 1–2 µm now; wells 10¹⁶–10¹⁷ cm⁻³ Lecture values; the notes give none (p. 10), and M08 uses them only as labelled illustrations (M08 §1)

The conversions that are not multiplications. A number density in cm⁻² becomes a charge density in C/cm² by multiplying by q, and that is the step most often skipped. An energy in eV is used directly as a potential in V: dividing by the electronic charge gives the same number. A per-unit-area capacitance in F/cm² becomes the capacitance of a real gate by multiplying by the gate area in cm², and gate areas start life in µm², so convert them with the 10⁻⁸ that squaring 10⁻⁴ produces. A temperature interval is the same in kelvin and in degrees Celsius, so the ΔT×2 of (7b.4) reads either way, but T itself must be absolute. A memory's size is counted in bits: the notes' "256 MB" and "4 GB" are the 256 Mb and 4 Gb generations, not a factor of eight larger.
